When they arrived, they bowed to Utpalavarṇā, asked after her health in the proper manner, and said, "While we were living in our homes, we were deceived by household affairs. Now, though we have gone forth, we have fallen under the power of desire and cannot free ourselves from the afflictions. Out of compassion for us, please teach us the sublime Dharma."

Utpalavarṇā said, "Ask about whatever you wish, any matter at all, past, future or present, and I shall explain it." The nuns replied, "Leave aside the past and the future for now, and teach us about the present. Please resolve our doubts."

Utpalavarṇā said, "Desire is like fire: it burns up mountains and valleys. It is like a spread of grass: it keeps spreading. Under the power of desire people harm and injure one another. Through it one falls for a long time into the three lower realms, with no time of release. Those who live in a household are attached to those they love, and they experience without cease the sufferings of separation from what they desire, of birth, aging, sickness and death, and of harassment by the king's laws. Body and mind are tormented, as though one had died and come back to life again. One is trapped by the household, and its bonds are tighter even than a prison.

"I too was born into a family of wandering ascetics (parivrājakas). Since my parents were of great and respected family, they gave me in marriage to a man of a great family of wandering ascetics, a man of great learning and versed in the Vedas of the wise. I became his wife, and he had a son by me.

"Later, after my parents-in-law had died, I became pregnant, and I said to my husband, 'I am with child, and my months are nearly full. If I give birth here it will be unclean and unpleasant, so I shall go to my parents' home to give birth.' He said to me, 'Very well.' So husband and wife set out together, taking our son as well, for my parents' home. When we had gone about halfway, my womb became uneasy, and in the middle of the night I gave birth to a son at the foot of a tree.

"My husband, who was sleeping apart, was bitten by a snake in the night. When I called to him in the night, he made no sound at all. When I looked at dawn, I found that my husband had been bitten by a venomous snake and had died, and his body was falling apart. Then I fainted and fell to the ground. My elder son, seeing that his father had been killed by the snake, cried out and wept, and at the sound of his weeping I came back to my senses. Carrying the elder son on my back and holding the little one in my lap, I went on, weeping. The road was deserted, I met no one at all, and I found no companion.

"On the way there was a great river. Because the river was wide and deep, I could not carry both children at once. I left the elder son on the near bank, carried the little one across to the far bank, and was coming back to fetch the elder son. But the boy, seeing his mother coming, went into the water and was swept away by the current. I went after him, but my strength was small and I could not catch him, and the current carried him off.

"Then, as I went back to the far bank, I found that the little son had been eaten by a wolf; only a little blood could be seen on the ground. There I fainted again, and when after a long while I came to, I set off along the road. As I went on I met a wandering ascetic who was close to my parents, a friend of theirs. That ascetic asked me, 'What has gone wrong, that you have become so afflicted?' I told him in full the story of what had happened. The ascetic wept, and when I asked him for news of my parents, he said, 'Recently a fire broke out in your parents' home, and your parents and everyone in the household were burned to death.' When I heard him say this, I fainted and fell to the ground.

"Then that ascetic lifted me up and took me to his home, where he loved and cherished me like a daughter. Later another wandering ascetic took me as his wife, and as I enjoyed the pleasures of love with him, I became pregnant. When my months were full and the birth was near, my husband was invited to someone else's home for beer, and while he was away I gave birth. I had shut the door tight and was giving birth when my husband, drunk on beer, knocked at the door. Since no one opened it, he became furious, broke down the door, came in, and beat me. When I said, 'I have given birth to a son,' he killed the child, fried it in butter, and made me eat it. I could not bring myself to eat my child's flesh, and when I did not eat it he beat me again and forced me to eat it. After I had eaten it, and my husband had sobered up, I thought to myself, 'I have no merit and my karma is evil.' I left my husband and fled to the country of Vārāṇasī.

"I was sitting at the foot of a tree outside Vārāṇasī. A young householder of that town, whose wife had recently died, had buried his wife's bones outside the city, and because he was attached to his wife, he was sitting by the bones, weeping. He came over to me and asked, 'What are you doing here all alone?'

"I told him my story just as it had happened, and that householder said, 'I will take you to my pleasure garden and make you my wife.' And I said, 'Very well.' Not long after we had come together as husband and wife, my husband was struck by illness and died.

"At that time, according to the custom of that country, husband and wife were united in life, and when the husband died, the wife too was put into the grave alive together with her husband. So I too was put into the grave there. In the night grave robbers came and dug open the grave, and the chief of the grave robbers, seeing that I was beautiful, made me his wife as well. Not long afterward, as the grave robber went out on his raids, he was killed by the king, and his fellow thieves brought back his bones and put me into the grave with him once again.

"I had been in the grave for three days when a wolf dug the grave open, and I got out of the grave. And I thought, 'What evil did I do in the past, that I have died many times like this and come back to life again? I once heard that a son of the Śākya clan has attained full awakening, that he is called the Buddha, and that he is renowned as knowing all of the past and the future. Now I shall go to him for refuge.' So I went toward the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, and from afar I saw the Tathāgata, like a tree in full flower, or like the moon among the stars.

"At that time the Blessed One, knowing by his unobstructed wisdom that the time had come to tame me, rose and came to meet me. Since I was naked, I was ashamed; I covered my breasts with my hands and sat down on the ground. Then the Blessed One said to Ānanda, 'Give this woman clothing to wear.' I put on the clothing, bowed my head at the Blessed One's feet, and said, 'Out of compassion for me, please grant me the going forth.' The Blessed One said to Ānanda, 'Entrust this woman to Prajāpatī and have her ordained.'

"Then Prajāpatī ordained me and taught me the Dharma of the Four Truths. As soon as I heard it, I strove with earnest diligence and attained the fruit of arhatship, and I came to understand all matters, past, future and present."

Those nuns said, "Tell us what evil deed you did in the past, that you experienced such a ripening." The nun Utpalavarṇā said, "Listen attentively, and I shall explain it to you.

"Long ago in the past, a very wealthy householder who had no son took another wife. Because he loved that wife dearly, before long she became pregnant, and when her months were full she gave birth to a boy, and husband and wife loved the child and held him dear. Then the senior wife thought, 'I am of a great and noble family, but since I have no son, I have no one to carry on my line. If this boy grows up, he will carry on the line and become master of the wealth and property. He will give me nothing, and I will be miserable.' Jealousy arose in her, and she resolved to kill him. She stuck a needle into the crown of the little boy's head, so that not even a wound could be seen.

"Not long after, the boy died. The junior wife fainted, and she accused the senior wife: 'You killed my son!' The senior wife denied it and swore this oath: 'If I killed your son, then in all my births may my husband be killed by a venomous snake! If I have a son, may he be swept away by the water and eaten by a wolf! May my body be put alive into the grave again and again, and may I eat my own child's flesh! May my parents' home catch fire and all be burned!' So she said. The senior wife who swore that oath at that time was I, and I have experienced every one of those things in my own body."

Those nuns said, "What merit did you make, that you saw the Buddha, that the Buddha came to meet you and ordained you, and that you have reached the end of saṃsāra?"

Utpalavarṇā said, "Long ago, here in Vārāṇasī, at the place called Ṛṣipatana, there lived many pratyekabuddhas, śrāvakas, and non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) endowed with the five clairvoyances. At that time some pratyekabuddhas went into the town for alms. A householder's wife saw them and was overjoyed; she offered them alms and paid them honor. Then that pratyekabuddha rose into the sky and displayed miracles: flames blazing from his body, water streaming from it, walking, lying down and sitting. And the householder's wife made the aspiration, 'In the future may I too attain such a state!' The householder's wife who made that aspiration at that time was I. For that reason, when I met the Tathāgata, my mind was liberated and I attained the fruit of arhatship. Yet although I have attained arhatship in this way, a hot iron spike pierces me from the crown of my head and comes out at the soles of my feet; such harm always befalls me."

Then some five hundred nuns of great and high family, hearing these words, shuddered in body and mind. They understood desires to be like a heap of glowing embers, their minds of desire were utterly exhausted, and they understood the suffering of household life to be worse even than a prison. All their stains exhausted, they entered meditative absorption at one and the same time and became arhats.

With one voice those nuns said to the nun Utpalavarṇā, "We were utterly overcome and trapped by desire, but through the Dharma you taught, we have reached the end of saṃsāra."

Then the Blessed One praised her, saying, "The nun Utpalavarṇā has taught well. Teaching the Dharma is one person teaching another. Having attained the fruit, they are true children of the Buddha." When he had said this, the great assembly rejoiced and was delighted.

This is called the chapter on the nun Utpalavarṇā, the twenty-fifth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove at Kalandakanivāpa. At that time the venerable Ānanda rose from his seat, arranged his robe, set his knee on the ground, joined his palms, and said this to the Blessed One:

"Please explain for what cause and for what condition these five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others, were the first to taste the nectar of the Dharma as soon as the Blessed One had turned the wheel of Dharma in the world."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "In the past too these five monks were the first to eat my flesh and were made happy by it, and so here too they were the first to taste the flavor of the Dharma and were liberated."

Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Please explain what these five monks did in the past."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago, before immeasurable, countless, inconceivable eons had passed, there arose here in Jambudvīpa a king called Shudolagarni, who held sway over some eighty-four thousand minor kingdoms of Jambudvīpa. At that time a soothsayer foretold that no rain would fall in that land for twelve years. When the king heard this, he was stricken with grief and despondent, and he thought, 'If a great famine like this comes, how will food for so many years be found for all these many people?' He gathered all the minor kings and ministers, and after they had deliberated, they reckoned up how much grain there was in the granaries and how many people there now were, and proclaimed by the sounding of a bell: 'Even if we reckon one handful for each person over the twelve years, it will not last twelve years.'

"After that the famine grew ever more severe, and many died. The king thought, 'What can I do so that the people may live?' With his queens and the palace retinue he went to stroll in the pleasure grove, and when they reached a resting place and the queens and all the many people had fallen asleep, he rose from his bed, bowed to the four directions, and made this vow: 'A famine has come upon this land, and all are without food. Because of this hunger I give up this body. After I die, may I be born as a great fish, and may all eat my flesh and be satisfied.' Having made this aspiration, he climbed a tree and from the top of the tree threw his body over a precipice. Dying there, he was born miraculously in a great river as a great fish five hundred yojanas long.

"At that time in that land five carpenters were on the riverbank gathering timber, and they saw the great fish. The fish spoke to them in human speech, saying: 'If you are hungry, cut my flesh and eat as much as you like. When you are satisfied, carry home as much as you can bear. Because you were the first to eat my flesh and be satisfied, later, when I have become fully awakened, you will be the first to taste the food of the Dharma. Tell the people of the land that all who are hungry should take my flesh as they please.'

"Then the five men cut the flesh, ate it, and told the people of the land, and as one told another, all the people of Jambudvīpa gathered, cut its flesh, and ate. When one flank was nearly consumed, the fish turned the other flank over of its own accord, and when the upper side was nearly finished, the underside likewise grew back. In this way, turning over top and bottom, it was cut and eaten by all for twelve years. Because all felt compassion for it, all those who had eaten its flesh were reborn, when they died, as gods in the heavens.

"Ānanda, the king who became the fish at that time, on that occasion, is I myself now. The five carpenters who first cut and ate my flesh are the five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others. The many people who later ate my flesh are the eighty thousand sons of the gods and my disciples who have been liberated. Because at that time I gave first to those five men and saved their lives, here too I taught them the Dharma first, and with the limbs of the Dharma body I quenched the fire of the three poisons."

Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said, and were delighted.

This is the chapter on Shudolagarni, the twenty-sixth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time, as the Blessed One was going for alms with Ānanda, some children were sitting by the roadside playing, making pretend houses, storehouses and jewels out of earth. Among them one boy saw the Buddha coming from afar and was overjoyed. Thinking to make a gift, he took a handful of the earth he had called the granary and tried to offer it to the Buddha, but the boy was too small and could not reach. He said to a friend, "You crouch down on the ground. I will stand on top of you and pour it into the bowl." His friend said, "I will do so," and the boy climbed onto his friend's shoulders and offered the handful of earth to the Buddha. The Buddha lowered his bowl and accepted the earth that was offered. Having accepted it, he gave it to Ānanda and said, "Make this earth into clay and plaster the vihāra with it."

"Ānanda, this small child has just now offered this to me with a joyful mind. Because it has been accepted and plastered on the temple, through that merit, a hundred years after I have passed into nirvāṇa, he will become a king named Aśoka. The other boy will become his minister. He will rule over Jambudvīpa, proclaim the qualities of the Three Jewels throughout all lands, and make vast offerings to the relics. He will build eighty-four thousand stupas in Jambudvīpa all at once."

Ānanda, glad and delighted, said this to the Blessed One: "What merit did the Tathāgata make in the past, that so many stupas will be built even for his relics?"

The Blessed One said, "Listen attentively and hold it well in mind, and I shall explain it to you. Long ago, countless eons in the past, there was here in Jambudvīpa a king named Prasenajit, who held sway over some eighty-four thousand minor kingdoms. At that time a buddha named Puṣya appeared in the world. That king, together with his ministers, prepared the four kinds of requisites and offered them to the Buddha and the Saṅgha of monks."

"At that time the king and the people of his land were always meeting the Buddha, bowing to him and serving him, but the other minor kings did not make merit at all. So that the minor kings of the borderlands and their peoples might make merit, the king thought, 'I will have images of the Buddha painted and give them to the minor kings.' He gathered many painters and commanded them, 'Paint images of the Buddha.' The painters went into the Buddha's presence and looked at his marks as they painted, but whenever they painted one mark, another came out unlike him, and the painters could not paint the image. Then the Buddha himself mixed the pigments and painted an image, and showed it to those painters. Eighty-four thousand images endowed with the marks were painted, and the king sent one image to each minor king, commanding, 'Let the king and all the people of the land worship this image with flowers, incense and every kind of offering, and bow down to it.' When those minor kings and their peoples saw the image of the Tathāgata, they were overjoyed, glad and delighted, and served it with honor.

"King Prasenajit of that time is I myself now. Through the merit of having painted eighty-four thousand images of the Tathāgata in this way and distributing them to all the minor kings and their peoples, wherever I was born I was born in the heavens as Śakra, lord of the gods. I gained a beautiful form, the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, and I have become fully awakened. And after I have passed into nirvāṇa, some eighty-four thousand stupas will be built for my relics."

When the Blessed One had said this, Ānanda and the great assembly were delighted and rejoiced.

This is the chapter on King Aśoka, the twenty-seventh.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the monks had entered the summer retreat each in their own place, and when the retreat was over, they came to where the Blessed One was. Because the Blessed One had not met those monks for a long time, he regarded them with loving affection, raised his hand marked with the thousand-spoked wheel, and asked the monks with courtesy, "Have you not been ill? Have you not been troubled in seeking alms?"

When the monk Ānanda saw that the Tathāgata, endowed with such great qualities, was showing courtesy to the monks, he was amazed and said this to the Blessed One: "The Blessed One is the lord of the world, of immeasurable qualities and inconceivable wisdom. Why then do you humble yourself and show courtesy to the monks?"

The Blessed One said this to Ānanda: "Long ago, countless, immeasurable, inconceivable eons in the past, there lived here in Jambudvīpa, in the land of Vārāṇasī, a householder skilled in earning a livelihood. He turned all the wealth he had into gold, made a golden vase, and hid it under the ground. In the same way, without ever enjoying his wealth, he turned all of it into gold, and after a long time the golden vases he had amassed one after another came to seven, and he hid them all under the ground. Later that householder was struck by illness and died, and because of his attachment to the golden vases he was born as a venomous snake and guarded that gold. After a long time had passed, that city no longer remained at peace, and it became deserted. That snake too, after many years, died, and was born again in that same body, lying coiled around the golden vases.

"In this way many tens of thousands of years went by, and in the last of those births he grew weary of that body and thought, 'Because of my attachment to this gold I have come to experience such an ugly body as this. What if I now offered it to a field of merit?' He went near the road, hid his body in a clump of grass, and thought, 'If people come, I will call to them.'

"Then the venomous snake saw a man coming along the road, and the snake called to him. The man heard the sound of someone calling and looked all around, but seeing nothing, he took to the road again and went on his way. The snake called again, saying, 'Hey! Come closer to me.'

"The man said, 'What do you want, calling me? You are extremely venomous, and you will kill me.'

"The snake said, 'If I meant to harm you, I could harm you even if you did not come near me.'

"At this the man was frightened and drew near to the snake. The snake said, 'I have a golden vase here. Can I entrust you with making merit for me? If you will not do it, I will harm you.'

"The man said, 'I can do it.' The snake led the man to the golden vases, handed him one golden vase, and said this: 'Take this gold and offer a feast to the Saṅgha. On the day the feast is offered I too will come, so come and fetch me.'

"Then the man carried the gold to the Saṅgha, offered the gold to the monk in charge of arrangements, and said, 'This is the gold of a venomous snake. Please offer a feast to the Saṅgha with this gold.' With that gold the feast was prepared. Then, having reckoned the day on which the feast would be offered, on that day the man took a basket and went to where the venomous snake was. The venomous snake saw the man coming and was overjoyed.

"The man put the snake into the basket and carried it off, and on the road he met another man. That man asked him three times, 'Where have you come from?' but the man carrying the snake said nothing at all. The snake grew angry and venom welled up in it, and it thought, 'What is wrong with this man, that when someone asks him three times he does not even give an answer?' Again it grew angry and venom welled up, but it checked itself and thought again, 'This man is making merit for my sake. If I remember the kindness of what he is doing for me and repay it with gratitude, no venom will arise. Even if this man, who is acting for my benefit, does something wrong, I should bear it patiently.'

"When they came to a lonely place, the snake said, 'Put me down on the ground for a while,' and, humbling itself before the man, it said, 'Now I regret my fault and confess it.' The man picked it up again, and when he reached the dwelling of the Saṅgha, it was the time of the Saṅgha's midday meal. The man scattered flowers over the Saṅgha, and the snake watched the flowers being scattered with a mind of faith. Then, when the Saṅgha had finished their meal and washed their hands and mouths, they taught the Dharma to the snake. The snake was overjoyed and offered the other six golden vases to the Saṅgha as well. Having done this meritorious deed, it then passed away. When it died, through that merit it was born as a god in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

"Ānanda, the man who carried the venomous snake as his load at that time is I myself. That venomous snake is Śāriputra. In time past, too, when I carried the snake as my load, the snake reviled me, and yet I humbled myself toward it; and so now too I humble myself toward the monks."

Ānanda and the others rejoiced at what the Buddha had said, and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the golden vase, the twenty-eighth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove at Kalandakanivāpa, together with countless monks. At that time there lived in that land a brahmin who was extremely poor and destitute and owned nothing at all. In his great want the brahmin asked everyone, "What can I do to come to have wealth in this very life?"

A man said to him, "Do you not know? The one called the Buddha, the Blessed One, has now come into the world, and he acts for the benefit of all sentient beings. His disciples, the four noble ones, Mahākāśyapa, Maudgalyāyana, Śāriputra, Aniruddha and the rest, are also always acting for the benefit of the poor and destitute. If you offer food to those noble ones with a mind of faith, those noble ones will grant you whatever you wish in this very life."

When the brahmin heard what the man said, he was overjoyed. He wandered everywhere, begging and working for hire, and when he had gained a little wealth he came back home, prepared food, invited the Saṅgha of noble ones, and offered them the meal, hoping for benefit in this very world. At that time the brahmin's wife, who was called Bliss, offered the meal to the Saṅgha and then took the precepts for a single day and night.

At that time King Prasenajit had gone out for a stroll, and on the road back he saw a prisoner who had committed a crime, tied to a tree beside the road. When the prisoner saw the king passing, he wept and begged for some food, and the king said, "I will give you some." But when he had gone back to the palace, he forgot, and only around midnight that night did he remember about giving food to the prisoner. He arranged to have food taken to him, but not a single person in the palace dared to go. To take food at midnight, out of fear of spirits, grahas and rākṣasas on the road, no one could go, for it meant staking one's life.

Then the king thought of that man's suffering and how he was tormented by hunger, and he felt great compassion for him. He proclaimed, "If anyone in this land of mine can take food to that prisoner, I will give him a thousand ounces of gold." But even so, no one was able to go.

At that time Bliss, the brahmin's wife, heard the news, and thought, "I have heard that whatever worldly person has taken the eight precepts for a single day and night is not overpowered by spirits, grahas and the like, and that no graha at all can harm them. So I will go wherever the king sends me." And she came forward. Then the king said to Bliss, "Take this food and give it to the prisoner who is at such-and-such a place, and I will give you a thousand ounces of gold." So he promised.

Then Bliss took the food as the king had commanded, and keeping her moral discipline in mind, she set out on the road. As she went along, outside the city she met a rākṣasī who haunted the road. That rākṣasī had just given birth to five hundred children and was tormented by hunger and thirst. Seeing Bliss coming, she set about devouring her, but because the laywoman's moral discipline was unimpaired, when the rākṣasī looked at her she grew afraid and said, "Laywoman, I beg a little of the food you are carrying." The laywoman gave her a small amount, and though what she gave was little, through the laywoman's power it multiplied into much, and the rākṣasī, mother and children, were fully satisfied.

Then the rākṣasī asked the laywoman, "What is your name?" and she said, "I am Bliss." "In that case, since you have saved me, mother and children, from starvation, there is a vase full of gold here where I live; take it on your way back. As you go on, my younger sister Alampa is also by the road, and you will meet her. Give her this message from me: 'Are you well? I too have safely given birth to five hundred children.'"

Then the laywoman set out on the road, and as she went along, before long she met Alampa. She gave Alampa her elder sister's message and told her the news that five hundred children had been safely born. Alampa was glad and said, "Laywoman, it is good of you to bring my elder sister's message and such pleasant news. Here where I live there is a vase full of gold; take it on your way back. Our brother is also by the road. If you meet him, give him the message of us two sisters, and tell him too the news of the birth of the five hundred children." So she instructed her, and the laywoman went on.

Then, before very long, she met the two sisters' brother. She gave him the message of the two women and the news that five hundred children had been safely born, and he too was overjoyed. He instructed her, "It is good of you to bring the news and message of my two sisters. Here where I stay there is a vase full of gold; take it on your way back."

Then the laywoman went on her way and came to the prisoner. She delivered the food, and on the way back she found the three vases of gold. The king too gave her a thousand ounces of gold as a reward, and so the poor brahmin and his wife became extremely rich in that land, with great wealth. They made merit all the more, and their treasures grew abundant.

The king too, knowing that this had come about through merit, made the brahmin a minister, and he became extremely rich. With a mind of faith he set about making merit all the more: he invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha and made great offerings to them. When the Blessed One taught them the Dharma, their minds were set free and they attained the fruit of stream-entry. That great assembly, together with Ānanda and the others, rejoiced at what the Buddha had said, and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the brahmin woman Bliss, the twenty-ninth.

The eighth section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak, together with a great Saṅgha of twelve hundred and fifty monks. At that time the Blessed One said, "I need an attendant to serve me, the venerable Ājñātakauṇḍinya."

Then Ājñātakauṇḍinya rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, joined his palms toward the Blessed One, bowed, and said, "Blessed One, I will serve you as you wish and carry your robe and alms bowl."

The Blessed One said to Kauṇḍinya, "You too are old and frail, and you yourself need someone to serve you. Kauṇḍinya, sit down on your seat."

In the same way Kāśyapa, Śāriputra, Mahāmaudgalyāyana and the others, some five hundred disciples in all, said, "We will serve the Blessed One," but he did not accept a single one of them.

Then the venerable Aniruddha considered which monk should serve the Blessed One, and saw that the Blessed One's heart was pleased with Ānanda in this way: just as, when the rays of the sun come through the eastern window of a multistoried house, they shine on the western wall and rest there, so the Blessed One's heart was turned toward Ānanda and pleased with him. When all the disciples had understood this, the venerable Śāriputra and Mahāmaudgalyāyana went to where Ānanda was. Coming before him, they exchanged many kinds of pleasing and delightful talk with Ānanda, and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana said this to the venerable Ānanda: "Venerable Ānanda, are you willing to serve the Blessed One? The Blessed One too would be pleased for you to serve him, and if you serve the Tathāgata it will be of great benefit to you."

"Venerable Śāriputra, I am not willing to serve the Blessed One. Why is that? The Buddha, the Blessed One, is hard to attend upon and hard to bear. Just as a blazing fire is hard to tend and hard to bear, so too the Buddha, the Blessed One, is hard to attend upon, and therefore I am not willing to serve the Blessed One."

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana said this to Ānanda: "Ānanda, the Blessed One's heart is turned toward you and pleased with you in this way: just as, when the rays of the sun come through the eastern window of a multistoried house, they shine on the western wall and rest there, so the Blessed One's heart too is pleased for you, Ānanda, to serve him. So be willing! If you serve the Tathāgata, it will be of great benefit to you."

"Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, if the Blessed One will grant me these three wishes, I am willing to serve the Blessed One. They are these: that the Blessed One not give me robes he no longer wears to use; that I not have to take the leftovers of food the Blessed One has not eaten; and that I be allowed to come before the Blessed One at any time, even when it is not the proper time. If these are granted, I am willing to serve the Blessed One."

Then the venerable Śāriputra and Mahāmaudgalyāyana, having made the venerable Ānanda willing to serve, went to where the Blessed One was. Coming before him, they said this to the Blessed One: "We have made the venerable Ānanda willing to serve the Blessed One, and he says, 'If the Blessed One grants me these three wishes, then I am willing to serve the Blessed One.'"

The Blessed One said, "Excellent! Maudgalyāyana, the monk Ānanda is wise. He sees that words of blame and reproach from his companions in the holy life could arise on account of these things, and so he asks this in order that his companions in the holy life will not say of him, 'The monk Ānanda serves the Teacher for the sake of clothing and food.' Maudgalyāyana, this is a wondrous and marvelous quality of the monk Ānanda.

"The monk Ānanda is wise: he knows the time and recognizes the right time. He knows the time in this way: he knows when it is the right time and when it is not the right time for the four assemblies to come to see the Blessed One; and further, when it is the right time and when it is not the right time for wandering non-Buddhist ascetics (tīrthikas) to come before the Blessed One; and further, that these wandering tīrthikas are bold enough to reply to what the Blessed One says, while those are not bold enough to reply; and that this food, hard or soft, will be agreeable and wholesome for the Blessed One, while that will not be agreeable and wholesome. Because he knows these things, Maudgalyāyana, this is a wondrous and marvelous quality of the monk Ānanda."

Then, when Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana had made the venerable Ānanda willing to serve the Blessed One, and the Blessed One too had praised him, saying, "Ānanda is wise; he knows the time and recognizes the right time," a doubt arose in the monks, and they said this to the Blessed One:

"For what cause and for what condition did this monk Ānanda become so skilled in knowing the right time?"

The Blessed One said to Śāriputra, "Long ago, beyond immeasurable, countless eons in the past, there arose a great king who held sway over some eighty-four thousand minor kingdoms of Jambudvīpa. At that time, in a city called Barute, there lived a brahmin called Nyagrodha. He was learned and full of knowledge, and skilled in medicine, the treatises and the stars, and so he served as that king's teacher. The eighty-four thousand minor kings honored him as they honored the great king himself, and they heeded his instructions. That brahmin's wealth rivaled even that of Vaiśravaṇa, but because he had no son he was always unhappy. Brooding inwardly on how he might come to have a son, he made offerings and prayers to Brahmā, Śakra, Īśvara, the sun and moon, the stars, and all the gods of the mountains, the waters and the forests.

"When twelve years had passed, his chief wife became pregnant. Wise women know for certain, as soon as a woman becomes pregnant, whether the child is a boy or a girl, and knowing that this one was a boy, they told the brahmin. When the brahmin heard what they said, he was overjoyed, glad and delighted, and he cared for his wife with still greater attention. Then, when nine months were complete, she gave birth to a baby boy, golden in color, with hair of deep blue, fine, beautiful and endowed with the marks. They held a birth feast, summoned a reader of signs, and after he had examined the marks they named the child Great Generosity.

"The boy gradually grew up, and they made separate residences for him for summer and winter, autumn and spring, and let him enjoy every pleasure. The boy was upright in mind and gifted with a wise intelligence, so that there was no art he did not know, the Vedas, the eighteen kinds of treatises and all the rest.

"Later Great Generosity said to his parents, 'I am going outside to stroll and see the sights.' His parents, saying, 'Our son is going out for a stroll,' had the streets swept, raised victory banners and pennants, scattered flowers, and laid out many furnishings such as burning incense. Great Generosity was seated on an elephant adorned with the seven kinds of precious things, and with bells ringing, drums beating, music and cymbals playing, surrounded by an entourage of some hundred thousand, he went out of the city. All the people of the land watched him without blinking from the upper floors of the houses on the left and right of the road, and proclaimed, 'His splendor and majesty are like those of Brahmā!'

"As he went along, Great Generosity heard beggars dressed in rags and carrying broken bowls calling out, 'We beg for a little something!' He asked them, 'Why are you so afflicted and miserable?' Some of them said, 'We have no father or mother, no brothers or sisters, no children and no kin or friends, and so we are miserable.' Some said, 'I have long been struck by illness and have not recovered, and so I am miserable.' Some said, 'Because I was careless I was robbed by bandits, and now I own nothing at all, so I go begging.' When Great Generosity heard them say this, he went on with tears streaming down.

"Then, a little farther on, he saw butchers killing animals, flaying their hides and chopping up the flesh. Great Generosity asked, 'Oh! What are these people doing?' They said, 'From the time of our fathers and grandfathers our family has done this work of killing, and if we did not do it we would not be able to live.' At this Great Generosity was astonished, and unable to bear the sight, he went on.

"Then, going farther on, he saw farmers plowing a field, and in the furrows left by the plow many worms had come up, and many birds were pecking up the insects and eating them. When he asked them what they were doing, they said, 'We plow the fields, and in the furrows where the earth has been turned we sow seed, which later grows abundantly. By this we make a good living, and we also pay our dues to the king.'

"Great Generosity was astonished at this too, and going farther on he saw hunters who had spread bird nets and set snares to catch birds. The creatures caught in the nets and snares were flapping their bodies and struggling but could not get free; they cried out pitifully and were in great terror. Seeing this, he asked, 'What are you doing?' They said, 'We too live by this kind of killing work, which is our family's trade.' When Great Generosity heard them say this, he was deeply dejected.

"Going on farther still, he saw fishermen who had set fishing nets and caught a great many fish, which lay piled in heaps on the ground, still twitching. When he asked, 'What are these people doing?' they said, 'Our family has no other work. We live by catching fish like this.'

"Having seen all these things, Great Generosity thought, 'Because they are poor and destitute, these beings commit such blameworthy deeds as killing in order to clothe and feed themselves. As soon as they die they will fall into the three lower realms, going from darkness into darkness, with no time of liberation.' Thinking this, he came back home, and brooding on it day and night, he was unhappy. He went to his father and said, 'Father, I have a request to make of you; please grant it.' His father said, 'Son, whatever you ask, I will do as you say.'

"'Father, earlier, when I went out to stroll, I saw that many people, all of them afflicted for the sake of clothing and food, kill one another, lie, seize what is not theirs, and commit all sorts of unwholesome deeds. Seeing this, I resolved to practice giving toward them. So please permit me to dispense freely from your great storehouses for the sake of the destitute.'

"His father said, 'All the storehouses and the many jewels I have amassed were made for your sake alone, so why would I not heed what you say?'

"Then Great Generosity, having received his father's permission, proclaimed to all the people of the land, 'Great Generosity is holding a great distribution of gifts. Let everyone gather and come!'

At that time the roads outside the city were filled with śramaṇas and brahmins, with the poor and destitute who had no protector, with the sick, and with others. They came from a hundred leagues away, from two hundred, three hundred, four hundred and five hundred, and from a thousand leagues away; some arrived from two thousand, three thousand, four thousand and five thousand leagues, and even from as far as ten thousand leagues. They gathered like clouds, and he gave to all of them as they wished: clothing to those who wanted clothing, food to those who wanted food, and jewels, gold, silver, vaiḍūrya, coral, mounts, carriages, palanquins, pleasure groves, fields, livestock and so on. He gave whatever they wished.

So he went on giving gifts, and when a long time had passed and a third of the storehouses had been used up, the keeper of the storehouses spoke to the brahmin: "Your son has been giving gifts from the storehouses, and one third is used up. Since no more than two thirds remain, please consider what we shall use for the gifts we exchange with others and for our trade goods."

The brahmin said, "Out of love for my son I cannot oppose him. Even if the storehouses are emptied, so be it."

Then again, after a long time had passed, when two thirds of the storehouses were used up and only about a third remained, the treasurer spoke to the brahmin again: "Two thirds of the storehouses are used up, and no more than about a third remains. What shall we use for sending gifts to one another and for our trade goods?"

The brahmin said, "My son is so very dear to me that I cannot oppose him; I dare not say anything. You do whatever is best for me, and make some excuse, as though you had no time to spare."

Having received the brahmin's permission, the keeper of the storehouses locked the doors of the storehouses and went off on business. After that, when beggars came to Great Generosity, at times the treasurer could not be found and the doors of the storehouses were not opened, and even when the treasurer did come, he did not give as they wished. So Great Generosity thought: "Since things have turned out like this, this is beyond the keeper of the storehouses; the instruction must have come from my parents. It is not right for a son to empty his parents' storehouses. What means can I use to obtain jewels as I wish, so as to be of benefit to many people?"

Having thought this, he asked people, "In this world, what work can one do to obtain jewels as one wishes, and to obtain great wealth and riches that will never run out?"

Some said, "If you plow many fields, you will have great wealth and riches."

Some said, "If you breed livestock and increase your herds, you will gain much wealth."

Some said, "If you trade on long journeys, you will gain much wealth."

Some said, "If you go out on the ocean and take precious gems, you will have many jewels."

When Great Generosity heard what they said, he declared, "Plowing fields, breeding livestock and going on long journeys to trade are not for me. Going out on the ocean alone is for me." Resolving to go out on the ocean, he said to his parents, "I shall take many jewels from the ocean and give gifts to the poor and destitute as they wish. Please permit me to go to the ocean to fetch jewels."

When his parents heard him say this, they were dismayed and said to their son, "In this world, the poor and destitute have no choice, so they set out staking their bodies and lives. What do you lack, that you think this way? If you want to give gifts, give away all the wealth there is in the house. You must not go to the ocean. In the ocean there are waves and great breakers and swirling whirlpools, sea monsters, venomous nāgas, rākṣasīs, those the color of mountain water, and many other exceedingly swift killers, so escape is hard. What do you lack, that you would stake your life? We will not listen to this. Stop thinking of it and stay here," they commanded.

When Great Generosity heard them command this, since he had not got his wish, he made a vow. He thought, "Until this wish of mine is fulfilled, I will lie face down on the ground before my parents and not get up." And he said, "Father and mother, please know this: until it comes about as I wish, I will not rise from lying face down here."

Then his parents were unhappy. They consulted with their relatives and friends, and told him too that there were many killers in the ocean. "In our ignorance we made offerings to all the gods for twelve years, and you, a god, were born as our son. Do not abandon us; eat your food!" For one day, two days, and on up to six days they counseled him with all manner of words, but he would not listen, and did not turn back from what he had said before.

His parents grew afraid and said further: "Ever since this son of ours was first born, he has never let go of anything he undertook until he had finished it. If we let him go to the ocean, he may yet come back; but if we do not let him go, then when seven days have passed it will be over with him at once. Now we must let him go." Thinking this, they went to their son, took him by the hand and said, "Son, get up and eat your food. We permit you to go as you wish."

Great Generosity then got up, ate food and drank drink. Then he went outside and proclaimed to many people, "I am going to the ocean to fetch jewels. Any of you who would like to go, come along with me. I will be the captain, and I will provide the supplies as well."

At that time about five hundred people agreed to go. Each of them prepared his supplies, they set a time for departure, and at the appointed time they set out on the road. As they set out, the young man's parents, the king, the princes, the ministers, and all the people of the land accompanied them and saw them off. His parents wept and bowed after him as he went.

After they had traveled many days' journey, they came into an empty wilderness, and at night on the road bandits attacked them and stole all their goods and wealth. Then, going on and on, they reached a city called Ngashupa. In that city he met a brahmin named Kapili, and going to that brahmin's house, Great Generosity asked the brahmin for three thousand gold coins.

At that time that brahmin had a daughter, golden in complexion, with dark blue-black hair, whom he would not give away even when the princes of some eighty-four thousand vassal kingdoms had all asked for her.

At that time Great Generosity went to that brahmin's door and asked, "Is the brahmin Kapili here?" As soon as the daughter, inside the house, heard Great Generosity's voice, she said to her parents, "This voice sounding at the outer door is my husband."

Then the brahmin came out to the door and met him, and thought of the young man, "This one is surely superior to others." When the young man asked for the gold, the brahmin agreed to give it, and holding the gold in one hand and leading his daughter by the other, he said, "Take this daughter as well; I give her to you as your wife."

Great Generosity said, "I am going to the great ocean to fetch jewels, and since there are very many obstacles in the ocean, there is no telling whether I will come back or not. I cannot take your daughter as my wife."

The brahmin said, "If you come back safely, take this daughter as your wife."

And Great Generosity said, "If I come back, I shall do so."

Then the brahmin provided the three thousand gold coins and all the other supplies he needed besides, and after that they went to the shore of the ocean. There they fitted out a ship, went aboard, and moored it with seven ropes. Then from inside the ship he spoke these words: "In the ocean there are these obstacles: black winds, rākṣasas, swirling waves and breakers, venomous nāgas, mountains that look like the color of water, sea monsters, and many other fearsome obstacles. So if there is any one of you who does not have the heart to go, turn back from here, lest you regret it later. If you have no regard for body and life, and are not attached to parents, wives, brothers and sisters, relatives and friends, then let us go. If we get there safely, the seven kinds of jewels will make you rich down to the seventh generation of your descendants, and your wealth will never run out." When he had said this, he cut one rope.

In the same way, for seven days, he said this once each day and cut one mooring rope each day. Then, catching the wind and hoisting the sail, they sped away like an arrow.

Then the merchants reached the Isle of Jewels. Great Generosity, being widely learned, was extremely skilled at assessing jewels, so he showed the merchants which jewels were good and which were bad, which heavy and which light, down to the price each was worth. "Jewels of such a color are light but of great value. Jewels like these are heavy but of little value; do not take them. If you take too many, the ship will be heavy and sink. If you take too few, you will have suffered for nothing in coming here. So this much is right." Having shown them the proper measure in this and other ways, they loaded the jewels onto the ship, and, untouched by any harm, they prepared to set off back home.

But Great Generosity would not go aboard the ship, and said to the merchants, "I will not turn back until I obtain the precious wish-fulfilling jewel from the palace of the nāga king."

Then the merchants wept, and, despondent with grief, they said, "We merchants came as your servants, O man, our captain. If you now leave us here and do not go, we will not get back home either, and all of us will be left wandering."

Great Generosity said, "Merchants, so that you come to no harm, I shall make an aspiration that you reach your own land." He took a bowl in his hand, made offerings to the four directions, and spoke these words: "I take jewels not only to benefit destitute beings, but so that by the merit of giving in this way I may attain buddhahood. If what I say is true, and it is to be accomplished as I aspire, may these merchants, together with their jewels, reach Jambudvīpa safely and without harm!" Having made this aspiration, he cut the rope, they hoisted the sail, and they went back safely to Jambudvīpa.

After they had gone, Great Generosity entered the water and went on. For seven days he sank no deeper than his knees. Then for another seven days he sank no deeper than his thighs. Then after another seven days he sank no deeper than his waist. For seven days the water reached his shoulders, and then for seven days he swam.

Then he reached the foot of a mountain, and clinging to the trees he climbed the mountain, reaching the summit in seven days. After he had spent seven days on the summit, in another seven days he went down to the water's edge. When he reached the water's edge, he saw that in the water there were golden lotuses, with venomous snakes coiled around the roots of the lotuses.

Then the bodhisattva sat down cross-legged and, with one-pointed mind, entered the samādhi of loving-kindness. He thought, "These venomous snakes too are experiencing such ugly bodies because of the condition of the aversion and jealousy they had in the past," and he generated an especially compassionate mind toward them. By this, all the venom of those venomous snakes was pacified, and the bodhisattva went on, stepping across the tops of the lotuses, and in seven days he had passed beyond those venomous snakes.

Then, going on further, he met rākṣasas, and the rākṣasas, sensing the smell of a human, searched everywhere. Then, as the bodhisattva regarded them with one-pointed loving mind, those rākṣasas asked him in gentle words, "Man, where have you come from, and where are you going?"

Great Generosity said to them, "I have come to seek the precious wish-fulfilling jewel."

Then those rākṣasas rejoiced greatly, thinking, "This is someone endowed with great merit." And they thought, "From here the nāga palace is still very far away. He will never get there and will suffer, so let us help him with our power and carry him there." So they carried him through the sky, and having gone four hundred leagues, set him down on the ground again.

Great Generosity then went on, and before long he saw a silver citadel. Thinking it must be a dwelling of the nāgas, he approached it. Outside, that citadel was encircled by seven rings of moats, and the moats were filled with terrifying venomous snakes. When Great Generosity saw them, he thought, "Because of the karma of their great aversion in the past, these venomous snakes are experiencing such terrifying bodies." Toward them, as toward an only child, he had a mind of love and compassion, and since he generated such a mind of loving-kindness, the snakes' venom was pacified. Then, treading on them, he went to the nāga citadel.

Two nāgas were coiled around that citadel, with their heads resting against the citadel gate. When those two nāgas saw Great Generosity, they raised their heads and were about to release their venom, but because Great Generosity generated a mind of loving-kindness, their venom was pacified, and they lowered their heads and lay on the ground. Great Generosity stepped on the nāgas' heads and went inside the citadel, and there inside the citadel he saw a nāga sitting in a celestial palace made of the seven kinds of jewels.

When that nāga king saw the bodhisattva, he was startled, and thought, "This dwelling of mine is encircled by seven rings of moats, and the moats are filled with venomous snakes, so no one other than nāgas and yakṣas can manage to come here. Who could this be?" He rose from his seat and went to greet him, paid him homage with respect, seated him on a jeweled throne, and served him all kinds of food.

When he had eaten the food, the nāga king asked, "Why have you come here, and how did you get here?"

The bodhisattva said, "The people of Jambudvīpa are poor and destitute, and for the sake of wealth, food and clothing they harm and injure one another in all sorts of ways, so that as soon as they die they fall into the three lower realms. I feel compassion for them, and to become their protector I have braved hardship on a long road and come to ask you, great king, for the precious wish-fulfilling jewel. Through the merit of benefiting all sentient beings with the precious wish-fulfilling jewel, I wish to attain complete and perfect awakening. So please grant me the jewel, and do not go against my wish."

The nāga king said, "The precious wish-fulfilling jewel is extremely hard to come by. If you have come for this purpose, stay here for one month, accept my offerings, and teach me the Dharma, and then I will give you the precious wish-fulfilling jewel."

And the bodhisattva said, "I shall do so."

Then each day the nāga king prepared and offered all kinds of food with a hundred flavors, and honored the bodhisattva with music. And for one month the bodhisattva taught him the Dharma topics of the applications of mindfulness. When he said, "I shall go back now," the nāga king was delighted, and untying the precious wish-fulfilling jewel from the crest of his head, he presented it to him and said, "Great being, you who are so steadfast in your vow to benefit beings will certainly attain awakening. When you become fully awakened, may I become your foremost attendant."

Then he asked the nāga king, "What power does this precious jewel have?"

He replied, "This jewel rains down everything that is needed, like rain, throughout an area two thousand leagues around."

The bodhisattva thought, "This precious jewel has great power, yet it cannot accomplish the great aim I have in mind." Having thought this, he went on his way, escorted by the nāga king and his retinue.

Then, going on further, he saw in the distance a citadel of blue vaiḍūrya. When he reached that citadel, it too was encircled by seven rings of moats, and the moats were completely filled with venomous snakes. Because the bodhisattva generated a mind of compassion and loving-kindness toward those venomous snakes, their venom was pacified, and treading on those snakes he went on. When he reached the citadel gate, two nāgas were lying at the citadel gate, and as the bodhisattva, with a mind of compassion and loving-kindness, pacified the nāgas' venom, he stepped on their heads and went inside the citadel.

At that time a nāga king was sitting in that citadel, in a celestial palace made of the seven kinds of jewels. Seeing the bodhisattva from far off, he thought, "This dwelling of mine is encircled by seven rings of moats, and the moats are filled with venomous snakes, so no one other than nāgas and yakṣas can manage to come here. Who could such a man be, and where has he come from?" He was amazed, and he went to greet him, paid him homage with respect, and seated him on a jeweled seat. Then he prepared and offered all kinds of food with a hundred flavors.

After the bodhisattva had eaten the food, the nāga king asked, "Why have you come here? What is your purpose?"

The bodhisattva said, "I have come to ask you, nāga king, for the precious wish-fulfilling jewel."

The nāga said, "The jewel is extremely hard to come by. If your wish is genuine, then, as I request, stay for two months, teach us the conduct of a bodhisattva, and accept our offerings, and then I will give you the jewel."

And Great Generosity said, "I shall do so."

Then for two months the nāga king prepared and offered all kinds of food with a hundred flavors, and honored him with all kinds of music. And for two months the bodhisattva taught them the Dharma discourse on the four bases of miraculous power. When he resolved to go back, the nāga king untied the precious wish-fulfilling jewel and presented it to him, saying, "Great being, you who are steadfast in your vow will certainly attain awakening. When you become fully awakened, I too shall be your attendant."

And he said, "May it be so."

When he asked, "How much power does this precious jewel have?" the answer was, "This precious jewel rains down whatever is wished for, like rain, throughout an area four thousand leagues around."

The bodhisattva in turn thought, "This jewel has great power, yet it cannot accomplish what I have in mind." And the nāga king escorted the bodhisattva outside the gate of his citadel.

Then, going on further, he saw in the distance a brilliantly shining citadel of gold. The bodhisattva went up to it, and when he arrived he saw that this citadel too was encircled by seven rings of moats, and that the moats were completely filled with venomous snakes. Once more the bodhisattva generated a mind of loving-kindness, so that the snakes' venom was pacified, and treading on them he went on. Around this citadel too two nāgas lay coiled, with their heads turned toward the outside of the gate. When they saw the bodhisattva, they raised their heads and released their venom, but because the bodhisattva generated a mind of compassion and loving-kindness, the venom was pacified, and stepping on their heads he went inside the citadel.

Inside that citadel, from his jeweled celestial palace, the nāga king saw the bodhisattva from far off and thought, "This dwelling of mine is encircled by seven rings of moats, and the moats are filled with venomous snakes, so no one apart from nāgas and yakṣas can manage to come here. Who is this man, and where has he come from?" Taking it as a marvel, he went out from afar to greet him, paid him homage with respect, and had him sit on a jeweled seat. Then he prepared and offered all kinds of food with a hundred flavors, and after he had eaten the food, he asked, "Who are you, and where have you come from?"

To this the bodhisattva replied, "I have come from Jambudvīpa. Because the beings of Jambudvīpa have little merit, they harm and kill one another in all sorts of ways for the sake of wealth, food and drink. Since they commit the ten unwholesome actions in full, as soon as they die they fall into the three lower realms and come to experience much suffering. I feel compassion for them, and in order to become their protector I have come to ask you, great king, for the precious wish-fulfilling jewel."

The nāga king said, "The precious wish-fulfilling jewel is not easy to come by. Man, if you have come placing your hope in me, stay four months here in my dwelling and accept my offerings, and then I will present you with the jewel."

And the bodhisattva said, "I shall do so."

Then for four months the nāga king prepared and offered all kinds of food with a hundred flavors, and honored him with music as well. The bodhisattva in turn explained and taught him Dharma discourses at length, so that the nāga king was overjoyed, and without depending on other nāgas or other yakṣas he himself provided all the requisites as the bodhisattva wished.

When four months had been spent in this way and the bodhisattva was preparing to leave, the nāga king untied the precious wish-fulfilling jewel from the crest on his head and presented it to the bodhisattva, and made this aspiration: "Great being, you who are steadfast in your vow will certainly attain awakening. When you become fully awakened, may I too become your foremost attendant and serve you with honor."

And the bodhisattva said, "May it be so."

He asked, "How much power does this precious jewel have?"

The nāga king said, "This precious jewel rains down jewels like rain throughout an area eight thousand leagues around; it rains down whatever is wished for."

At this the bodhisattva was at last delighted, and thought, "The expanse of Jambudvīpa is no more than seven thousand leagues. This precious jewel will fulfill my hope." Having thought this, he tied all three precious jewels he had obtained, the earlier ones and the later one, into the hem of his garment, and went to the citadel gate. The nāga king and his retinue escorted him, and then they went their separate ways.

When the bodhisattva had gone some distance, he took the precious jewels in his hand and made this aspiration: "If this jewel is truly a precious wish-fulfilling jewel, may I fly through the sky!" No sooner had he said this than he flew up into the sky, escaped the obstacles of the ocean, and reached the ocean shore. There, as he rested a little and recovered from his weariness, he fell asleep on the road.

At that time the lesser nāgas of the ocean conferred, saying, "In this ocean there are no more than these three precious jewels, and this man has carried off all three. Since these jewels have such virtues, we need them too, so let us steal them back." Having conferred in this way, while the bodhisattva lay asleep, the nāgas stole the precious wish-fulfilling jewels back.

When the bodhisattva woke and looked, the precious jewels were gone, and he thought, "The nāgas of the ocean have certainly stolen them. With whatever power I have, I will bail out the water of the ocean and dry it up." And he made a vow: "Until I get the jewels back, I will not go back empty-handed." He went to the ocean shore, took up a tortoise shell, and with both hands began bailing out the water of the ocean.

At that time the god of the ocean, knowing his intention, said to him, "This ocean is deep and wide, three hundred and thirty thousand leagues in extent. If even all the people of Jambudvīpa together could not dry up the ocean, what need is there to speak of you managing it alone? It is pointless."

The bodhisattva said, "If one person does a task with all his heart, there is nothing that cannot be accomplished. When I have found that jewel of mine, I intend to bring benefit and happiness to all beings, and by that merit I shall attain full awakening. If my mind does not lose heart in this, why should I not be able to dry up this ocean?"

At that time the god Viṣṇu and others saw from afar the bodhisattva, alone, bailing out the water of the ocean with body and mind in accord, undertaking hardships for the sake of many beings. Thinking, "We too shall help the bodhisattva," they passed the word from one to another, and all of them came to the bodhisattva's side. Seeing the bodhisattva bailing out water with a vessel, all those gods took off their own divine garments and dipped them in the water. When they wrung them out once, the water of the ocean went down by forty leagues. When they dipped them again, a second time, it went down by eighty leagues.

When they dipped them a third time, it went down by a hundred and twenty leagues. At this those nāgas were terrified, and they came before the bodhisattva and said, "Here, take them! Do not dry up the water of the ocean!" And the bodhisattva let it be.

Then the nāgas said to the bodhisattva, "What do you need this precious jewel of yours for?"

"I need it to benefit sentient beings."

The nāgas said, "If you need it to benefit sentient beings, there are many sentient beings in the ocean as well. Why carry it away?"

He said, "Those who dwell in the ocean are sentient beings too, but they do not have the suffering of want. The sentient beings of Jambudvīpa harm and injure one another for the sake of wealth, and since they commit the ten unwholesome actions in full, when they die they are all born among the beings of hell. Out of compassion for them, I need this jewel."

At this the nāgas brought out the precious jewel and presented it back to him.

When the god of the ocean saw the bodhisattva's steadfast diligence, he thought, "This one will certainly attain awakening," and made this aspiration: "When he becomes fully awakened, may I too become his foremost attendant."

Then the bodhisattva, having got the jewel back, flew up into the sky above, and arriving at the place, asked, "Have the merchant companions who left earlier gone on? Did they arrive safely?" The merchant companions had indeed arrived safely, and they rejoiced in one another, and his companions regarded it as a wonder and a marvel.

Then he went to the door of the brahmin Kapili. When they heard that the bodhisattva had returned from the ocean, all of them came out to the door to welcome him, overjoyed, and held a great feast for him and his merchant companions. When the bodhisattva came into the house holding the jewel, all the storehouses of that brahmin became completely full.

Then the brahmin Kapili adorned his daughter with all kinds of jewels, sent for a jeweled water vessel, washed the bodhisattva's body and feet, and, taking his daughter by the hand, entrusted her to the bodhisattva. When the bodhisattva accepted her, the brahmin was overjoyed, and he adorned with jewels five hundred maidservants for his daughter and five hundred elephants as well, and with music sent them along to the bodhisattva.

Meanwhile, after they had been parted from their son, Great Generosity's parents wept constantly, so that both parents went blind. When the son returned from the ocean and met his parents, the son paid homage to them, and taking hold of his hand they recognized that it was their son. "Great Generosity, after you left us and went away, we even went blind. Having gone all the way to the ocean, what did you find?"

Great Generosity placed the precious jewel in his father's hand and said, "I found this." His father took it in his hand and held it, and said, "I already had plenty of stones like this in my treasury before. Why did you need to stake yourself on such hardship?"

Then the bodhisattva took the jewel back, and merely by his wiping his parents' eyes with it, both his parents' eyes saw even more clearly than before, like clouds swept away by a strong wind. Then both his parents rejoiced and said, "This truly is a precious jewel; it has made up for the hardship."

Then Great Generosity took the jewel and made this aspiration: "If you are truly the precious wish-fulfilling jewel, let a seat of the seven kinds of jewels appear beneath my parents, with a jeweled canopy spread above them!" No sooner had he made this aspiration than it came about just so, and all the storehouses of the king also became full.

Then the king had a messenger mount an elephant able to travel a hundred thousand leagues, and proclaim to all the people of Jambudvīpa: "Great Generosity has found the precious wish-fulfilling jewel in the ocean. Seven days from now, that jewel of virtues will rain down precious jewels and so on, clothing, food and all the requisites you need, like rain, so each of you take whatever you please."

When this had been proclaimed everywhere, Great Generosity bathed, put on fresh clothes, planted a banner upright on a level plain, fastened the precious wish-fulfilling jewel to the top of the banner, took a bowl in his hand and made this aspiration: "The people of Jambudvīpa are all poor and destitute, and they must be helped. You are the precious wish-fulfilling jewel; if this is not false, rain down like rain, as they wish, all the various things that each of them needs!"

No sooner had he said this than a black wind arose from the four directions, and at once swept away all the filth. A light, gentle drizzle fell, so that no dust rose, and after that, first, all kinds of food and drink with a hundred flavors rained down like rain. Then grain rained down like rain. Then clothing rained down like rain. Then jewels rained down like rain, until Jambudvīpa was completely filled with heaps of precious jewels, and jewels became like gravel and potsherds.

When the bodhisattva had thus satisfied everyone, he admonished the people of Jambudvīpa with these words: "The beings of Jambudvīpa kill one another for the sake of clothing, food and wealth, and torment one another in all sorts of ways, and so they do nothing but evil, unwholesome deeds. When they die they fall into the three lower realms, and go from darkness into darkness. Out of compassion for them I disregarded the obstacles of the ocean and many hardships, and in order to make you happy I brought precious jewels from the ocean. Now none of you lacks anything at all; you are happy. So gather in your body, speech and mind and apply yourselves diligently to the path of the ten wholesome actions." In this way he taught and urged them by all kinds of skillful means.

Having taught and urged them in this way, he made them practice the path of the ten wholesome actions earnestly, so that when those people of Jambudvīpa died, they were born as gods in the higher realms.

"At that time, on that occasion, his father, the brahmin Nyagrodha, is now my father Śuddhodana. The one who was his mother is my mother, Lovely Goddess. Great Generosity was I. The nāga who dwelt in the silver palace is Śāriputra. The nāga who dwelt in the vaiḍūrya palace is Maudgalyāyana. The nāga who dwelt in the golden palace is Ānanda. The god of the ocean is Aniruddha. When this Ānanda was a nāga king, he too served me, and he was skilled in timing; even until now he is skilled in timing, and his three wishes too have been granted just as he desired."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, Ānanda was delighted and overjoyed, and setting his knees upon the ground he said, "For as long as I live I shall serve the Tathāgata."

When the Blessed One had said this, among the great assembly some attained the fruit of stream-entry and so on up to arhatship. Some became pratyekabuddhas. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. Some attained the ground of non-returning, and rejoicing, they were delighted.

This is the thirtieth chapter, on Great Generosity's entering the ocean.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Nyagrodha grove. At that time, as the Blessed One sat surrounded by Brahmā, Śakra (Indra) and others, the whole great assembly marveled at the Blessed One's body, with its major and minor marks and its color like gold, and they praised and extolled it.

Since the Blessed One thus surpassed the entire assembly and had become the supreme and foremost of all, the monks were puzzled and asked the Blessed One about it. The Blessed One said, "It is not only now that I have surpassed the whole assembly and become supreme and foremost. In times long past as well I surpassed all these and became foremost."

Then the monks asked him to explain how he had become foremost in the past, and the Blessed One spoke as follows:

"Long ago, in times past, countless, measureless, inconceivable eons ago, there was here in Jambudvīpa a great king who ruled over eighty-four thousand vassal kings, and he had five hundred sons. Some time later his chief queen gave birth to a son. He was golden in color, his hair was like sapphire, on his palms he had the mark of a wheel with a thousand spokes, on the sole of his left foot there was the figure of a horse, and on the sole of his right foot the figure of an elephant. Such a meritorious son was born, and he was given the name Mirror Face.

"When the great king was struck by illness and drew near the time of his death, the ministers asked him, 'Among these sons, whom shall we install as regent? On whom shall we confer the royal empowerment?' The king commanded, 'Whoever has these ten qualities and marks of mine, make him king and empower him.' What are the ten? They are these: the color of his body is like gold and his hair is sapphire blue; the palms of both his hands bear the mark of the wheel; on the sole of his right foot there is the figure of an elephant; on the sole of his left foot there is the figure of a horse; when he puts on the royal garments they fit his body, neither too large nor too small; when he sits on the royal throne he is endowed with splendor and might; the vassal kings show him great reverence and service, and the queens and palace women delight in him and bow to him; when he is led to the shrine of the gods, the gods bow to him in return; by the power of his virtues and merit he brings down a rain of the seven precious things and satisfies the minds of all; and he is born of the chief queen. These are the ten. 'If he has these qualities, confer the royal empowerment on him,' he commanded, and then impermanence became manifest and he passed away.

"Then the princes and ministers gathered, and they examined the princes from the eldest to the youngest to see which of them had the ten qualities. None of the other princes had a body the color of gold. Their hair was not sapphire blue. Their palms did not bear the mark of the wheel. The soles of their feet did not bear the figures of an elephant and a horse. The royal garments did not fit them. When they were placed on the royal throne, they rose up as if frightened. The vassal kings and ministers did not show them respect or bow to them, and the queens and palace women did not delight in them or bow to them. When they were led to the shrine of the gods, the gods did not bow to them. They were not able to bring down a rain of jewels. Nor were they sons of the chief queen. Of the five hundred princes, not a single one had even one of the marks.

"Only the youngest of the sons had a body the color of gold, hair the color of sapphire, and both hands bearing the mark of the wheel. On the soles of both his feet were the figures of an elephant and a horse. The garments fit his body. When he was placed on the royal throne, his splendor and majesty were great. All the vassal kings bowed to him with respect, and the queens and palace women also bowed to him with delight. When he came to the shrine of the gods, the gods bowed to him. As soon as he said, 'Let jewels fall!' they fell like rain. And he had been born of the chief queen among the queens of the palace. Since he possessed the marks in full, the vassal kings and ministers empowered him as king.

"On the fifteenth day of the month, when the sun first rose, a golden wheel with a thousand spokes, a full league across, came from the east. He knelt before it and said, 'If I possess the qualities and am to become king, may this wheel come close to me!' And it came close to him through the sky.

"The precious elephant came from Gandhamādana, the mountain of fragrance, its tail adorned with jewels. Mounted on it, the king flew through the sky and circled the four continents in half a day. When it went on foot, wherever its footprints fell turned to golden sand.

"The precious horse was blue in color, with a red tail and mane, and the seven precious things fell from it. Mounted on it, the king circled the four continents in about the time it takes to eat a single meal, without tiring or growing weary.

"The precious jewel shone with rays of light day and night, illuminating everything up to a hundred and twenty leagues with its light. It rained down the seven precious things like rain and gave them to all.

"The precious queen was virtuous and beautiful, and she gladdened the king's mind. The precious householder supplied the king with whatever he needed of the seven precious things, just as he wished, and made them inexhaustible. As for the precious minister, when the king divided the fourfold army, at a mere glance from him the hosts of the army assembled, endowed with skill and might.

"Possessing the seven precious things in this way, the king thought, 'Obtaining such means as these has come from my past merit, so I must act in such a way that the royal line is not cut off.' With this thought, he bathed in scented water, put on new and clean clothes, took a censer in his hand, knelt on the ground facing the east, and spoke these words: 'Noble beings who dwell in the east, please come, as I request!' When he had said this, some twenty thousand pratyekabuddhas came from the east to the king's palace. He made the same request toward the south, the west and the north, and from each of these, too, some twenty thousand pratyekabuddhas came to the king's palace. Then the king and his ministers prepared the four requisites and made offerings.

"Then the eighty-four thousand vassal kings asked leave to return to their own lands, and as they took their leave and bowed, the vassal kings asked for a share of the noble ones, saying, 'We too wish to make offerings and earn merit.' And the king and the ministers made offerings to forty thousand pratyekabuddhas, as they wished, for eighty-four thousand years. When they died, they were born as gods in the higher realms.

"Monks, King Mirror Face of that time is now I. That king's father is my father Śuddhodana. That queen is my mother, Lovely Goddess. The five hundred princes are the five hundred of the Śākya clan. At that time too I became the chief and supreme among all, and now that I have become a buddha, I have again become the supreme and foremost, endowed with the marks."

The great assembly rejoiced in what the Buddha had said and was delighted.

This is the thirty-first chapter, on King Mirror Face.

The ninth section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time Devadatta, though he had gone forth, was attached to gain, and so he committed deeds of immediate retribution: he rolled down a boulder to crush the Buddha, he drove a splinter of wood into his foot, he let loose a mad elephant, he created a schism in the harmonious Saṅgha, and he killed a nun whose defilements were exhausted.
