Having done these unwholesome deeds, he was afraid of their ripening in his next life, and he went to the six non-Buddhist (tīrthika) teachers and questioned them. The six teachers taught him a doctrine of wrong view: that even if one does evil it does not become evil, and even if one does good it does not become merit. He put his faith in this and cut off his roots of virtue.

At that time Ānanda, out of affection for his brother, was deeply unhappy, and weeping he said to the Blessed One, "Deluded Devadatta has committed unwholesome and reprehensible deeds, and having cut off his roots of virtue, he brings disgrace even upon the Śākya clan."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only in this life that Devadatta has cut off his roots of virtue for the sake of gain and fame. In times past too, for the sake of gain and fame, he committed deeds that cost him his body and his life."

At this the monks were puzzled, and Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "How was it that in times past too Devadatta committed deeds that cost him his body and his life for the sake of gain and fame?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Ānanda, long ago, in times past, countless, measureless, inconceivable eons ago, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land of Vārāṇasī, there lived a brahmin called Hayi. He took a wife from a family of equal caste, and before long his wife became pregnant. When nine months had passed she gave birth to a boy. They held a great birth feast and named the boy Good Seeker, and entrusted him to a nurse to raise.

"Then she became pregnant again. Whereas before she had been gentle and kind, once she was pregnant she became fierce and wrathful. When nine months had passed she gave birth to a boy, and because the mother had thus changed from before, they named the boy Evil Seeker, and entrusted him to a nurse to raise.

"Later, when the two boys had grown up, the two brothers went to the ocean to fetch jewels. Each of them found five hundred attendants and companions, and they set out on the road. Because the road was long, their provisions ran out seven days' journey short of their goal, and they were close to losing their lives. Good Seeker and his merchant companions were in desperate straits; they all looked about and called upon the gods, making offerings to them.

"Then in that empty wilderness they saw, far off, a fine tree, and they all went up to the tree. At the foot of the tree there was also a fine spring, and the merchants drank from the spring and said, 'This is the sign that our offerings to the gods have been accepted.' Then a god spoke to them in visible form and instructed them: 'Cut the branches of the tree, and everything you desire will come forth.'

"So they cut one branch, and delicious drinks came forth. Then they cut another branch, and all kinds of food with a hundred flavors came forth, and they all ate until they were satisfied. Then they cut another branch, and clothes of every kind came forth. Then they cut yet another branch, and all kinds of precious jewels came forth, and all their loads were filled.

"Then Evil Seeker and all his company also arrived there, and seeing that those who had come first had filled their loads with all kinds of jewels by cutting the branches, they thought, 'If these earlier ones got jewels enough even from the branches, then if we pull up the roots of this tree we shall get a great many jewels of every kind.' With this thought, they set about digging up the roots of the tree.

"Then Good Seeker was displeased and said, 'We were destitute and about to lose our lives, and this tree saved our lives, so do not cut this tree!' But Evil Seeker would not listen, and he cut the roots of the tree.

"Then Good Seeker, not wishing even to see this, went away with his loads. After he had gone, five hundred rākṣasas emerged from the roots of the tree and devoured Evil Seeker and all the merchants.

"Ānanda, at that time, on that occasion, Good Seeker is now I. His father is now my father Śuddhodana. His mother is now my mother, Lovely Goddess. Evil Seeker is now Devadatta. Ānanda, it is not only now that Devadatta does me harm: in all his lives he has harmed me for the sake of gain and fame. And although I have taught him the Dharma that would benefit him and have done him good, Devadatta holds me as his enemy."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the great assembly rejoiced and was delighted.

This is the thirty-second chapter, on Good Seeker and Evil Seeker.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain, together with a great Saṅgha of monks. At that time the venerable Ānanda saw that although Devadatta schemed by all kinds of means to harm the Tathāgata, the Tathāgata regarded Devadatta and Rāhula without any difference and always looked on him with compassion. He asked the Blessed One about this, and the Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only in this present time that Devadatta has harmed me. In other lives too he has harbored malice toward me."

The monks were puzzled, and Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "How did Devadatta harm the Blessed One in other lives?" And the Blessed One said to Ānanda:

"Long ago, in times past, immeasurable, countless, measureless, inconceivable eons ago, there was here in Jambudvīpa a king called Jewel Armor, who ruled over five hundred vassal kings. Although he had some five hundred queens and palace women, he had no son. He prayed to all the gods, the gods of the sun and moon and the rest, but though many years passed he still had no son, and he grew unhappy. He thought, 'I do not have even a single son. When my life comes to an end, the five hundred vassal kings will not obey one another; they will all fall into conflict, and the land will be in turmoil.' With this thought he sank into gloom and sat with his cheek resting on his hand.

"At that time a god, knowing the king's thoughts, instructed him in a dream: 'In the grove outside the palace there are two sages, and one of them has a body the color of gold. Go to him and make your request, and he will be born as your son.' As soon as he had been told this, the king awoke from sleep and went to look for where the two sages were. Meeting the sage, he made this request: 'I have no one to carry on the royal line, so be born into my family and restore the line.' And the sage said, 'I shall do so.' The other sage also said, 'I too shall be born into your family,' and the king, overjoyed, said, 'May you also be born into my family!'

"Then the king returned home. Some time later the golden-colored sage died and was conceived in the womb of the chief queen. Being a knowledgeable woman, she knew that she was with child, and knew it was a boy, and she said to the king, 'I am certainly carrying a son.' And the king provided for the queen's care. When nine months had passed she gave birth to a boy, golden in color, with hair of sapphire blue, endowed with the marks. The king, overjoyed, held a birth feast. The soothsayer read the marks, and the boy was given the name Good Purpose.

"Then another queen also became pregnant. The other sage, too, had died and was conceived in that queen's womb. Again a birth feast was held, and when the soothsayer had examined the marks, the boy was given the name Evil Purpose.

"Because the king loved his sons and held them dear, he made separate residences for them for summer, winter, autumn and spring. When the boys grew up, they became learned in all the treatises, the eighteen sciences and the rest.

"Later Good Purpose said to the king, 'I would like to go outside, to stroll and see the sights.' His parents, saying, 'Our son is going out for a stroll,' had the streets swept, raised victory banners and pennants, and scattered flowers. They set out the arrangements, with incense and fragrant smoke and the like, and seated Good Purpose on an elephant adorned with the seven precious things. With bells ringing, clay drums beating, and music and cymbals playing, surrounded by some hundreds of thousands of attendants, he went outside the city. All the people of the land, on the upper stories of the houses to the right and left of the road, gazed at him without blinking, and proclaimed, 'His majesty and splendor are like Brahmā's!'

"As he went on, he heard some beggars dressed in rags and holding broken bowls calling out, 'Give us a little!' Good Purpose asked them, 'Why are you so afflicted and miserable?' Some of them said, 'We are miserable because we have no parents, no brothers or sisters, no children and no kin or friends.' Some of them said, 'We have long been struck by illness and do not recover, and so we are miserable.' Some of them said, 'When we held a wedding we were plundered by robbers, and we have nothing at all left, so we go begging.' When Good Purpose heard them say this, he shed tears and went on.

"A little farther on he saw some butchers killing animals, flaying their hides and chopping up their flesh. Good Purpose asked, 'Oh! What are these men doing?' They said, 'Our fathers and forefathers, generation after generation, have done this work of slaughter. If we did not do it, we could not live.' At this Good Purpose felt ashamed and, unable to bear the sight, went on.

"Then, going a little farther, he saw some farmers plowing a field. Behind the plow many worms appeared, and many birds and venomous snakes gathered and ate them. He asked, 'What are these men doing?' They said, 'We plow the field, and in the furrows where the soil has been broken up we sow seed. Later it grows abundantly, and by this we live and also pay the king's tax.'

"Good Purpose was ashamed at this too and went on a little farther. He saw hunters who had spread bird nets and set snares to catch birds, and creatures caught in the nets and snares, beating their bodies and struggling but unable to break free, crying out piteously, in great terror. He asked, 'What are you doing?' and they said, 'We too, generation after generation, live by doing this kind of killing work.' When Good Purpose heard them say this, he was deeply dismayed.

"Going on a little farther again, he saw fishermen who had set fishing nets and caught a great many fish, which they had heaped in piles on the open ground, still twitching. He asked, 'What are these men doing?' and they said, 'We too do no work other than that of our forebears, and so we live by catching fish like this.'

"Having seen such things, Good Purpose thought, 'Because these beings are poor and destitute, they commit such reprehensible deeds as killing for the sake of clothing and food. As soon as they die, they will fall into the three lower realms, going from darkness into darkness, with no time of liberation.' With this thought he returned home, and brooding over it day and night, he was unhappy. He went to his father and said to him, 'Father, I have a request to make of you; please grant it.' His father said, 'Son, what are you saying? I shall do whatever you say.'

"'Father, when I went outside for a stroll before, I saw many people all afflicted for the sake of clothing and food, killing one another, lying, using force against one another, and committing all kinds of unwholesome deeds. Since I wish to give to them, Father, please allow me to use the great treasury as I see fit for the needy and destitute.' His father said, 'All the jewels and the many treasuries I have amassed were gathered for your sake alone, so why should I not grant what you ask?'

"Then, having received his father's permission, Good Purpose announced to all the people of the land, 'Good Purpose is holding a great giving, so let everyone gather and come!' At that time renunciants and brahmins, the poor and destitute, the helpless, the sick and others filled the roads outside the city. They came from a hundred, two hundred, three hundred, four hundred, five hundred, and a thousand leagues away; some came from two thousand, three thousand, four thousand, five thousand, and ten thousand leagues away. They gathered like clouds, and he gave to all of them whatever they wished: clothes to those who wanted clothes, food to those who wanted food; jewels, gold, silver, vaiḍūrya, coral, mounts, carriages, palanquins, pleasure gardens, fields, cattle and so on. He gave whatever they wished.

"He continued giving in this way, and after a long time had passed, a third of the treasury was exhausted. The treasurer said to the king, 'Since the prince has made gifts from the treasury, a third of it is exhausted, and only about two thirds remain. Please consider what we shall use for the gifts we exchange with others and for trade goods.' The king commanded, 'Because I love my son, I cannot refuse him. Even if the treasury becomes empty, it does not matter.'

"Then, after another long time had passed, two thirds of the treasury were exhausted and only about a third remained, and the treasurer again said to the king, 'Now about two thirds of the treasury are exhausted and only about a third remains. What shall we use for sending gifts to one another and for trade goods?' The king instructed him, 'My son is so very dear to me that I cannot refuse him and dare not speak to him. You find whatever means is best, and make the excuse that you are busy and have no time.'

"Having received the king's instruction, the treasurer locked the door of the treasury and went off on business. After that, when beggars went to Good Purpose, sometimes the treasurer could not be found and the treasury door would not be opened, and even when the treasurer did come, he would not give as was wished. So Good Purpose thought, 'This state of affairs is not something the treasurer could have decided on his own; it must have come from my father's instruction. Even so, it is not right for a son to empty his father's treasury. What means can I now find so as to obtain jewels as I wish and benefit many people?'

"With this thought he asked people, 'In the world, what work can one do to gain great wealth that never runs out?' Some said, 'If you plow fields on a large scale, you will have great wealth.' Some said, 'If you breed cattle and make a profit, you will gain great wealth.' Some said, 'If you trade for profit over long roads, you will gain great wealth.' Some said, 'If you go out on the ocean and fetch precious jewels, you will have many jewels.'

"When Good Purpose heard what they said, he said, 'Plowing fields, breeding cattle and going on long roads as a trader are not for me. Only going out on the ocean is for me.' Resolving, 'I shall go out on the ocean,' he said to his parents, 'I shall fetch many jewels from the ocean and give to the poor and destitute as they wish. Please allow me to go to the ocean to fetch jewels.'

"When his parents heard him say this, they were alarmed and said to their son, 'In the world, the poor and destitute have no choice; they stake their bodies and lives and go. What do you lack? If you have such a mind to give, give away all the wealth there is in the house. But you must not go to the ocean. In the ocean there are waves, swirling whirlwinds, sea monsters, venomous nāgas, rākṣasīs, hidden rocks the color of the water, and many other fearsome obstacles, so that it is hard to come through safely. What do you lack, that you would stake your life? We will not listen to this, so stay here and do not think of it.'

"When Good Purpose heard them instruct him so, since things had not turned out as he had intended, he made a vow, thinking, 'Until this intention is fulfilled, I shall lie face down on the ground before my parents and not get up.' And he said, 'Father and Mother, please know this: until my intention is fulfilled, I shall not get up from lying face down here.'

"Then his parents, unhappy, consulted with their kin and friends, who spoke of the many fearsome obstacles in the ocean. 'We, in our bewilderment, worshipped all the gods for twelve years, and someone like you was born as our son. Do not abandon us; eat your food!' From one day, two days, up to six days they pleaded with him in all kinds of words, but he would not listen and did not turn back from what he had said before.

"His parents, alarmed, consulted together and said, 'From the time our son was first born until now, he has never given up anything he set out to do until he has carried it through. If we let him go to the ocean, he may perhaps come back. If we do not let him go, once seven days have passed he will die at once. So now we must let him go.' Having agreed on this, they went to their son, took him by the hand, and said, 'Son, get up and eat. We allow you to go as you wish.' Good Purpose got up, ate food and took drink.

"Then he went outside and proclaimed to the people, 'I am going to the ocean to fetch jewels. Whoever of you wishes to go, come along with me! I shall be the captain, and I shall provide the supplies.' At that time some five hundred merchants of that land said, 'We too will go to the ocean to fetch jewels,' and they all gathered and set out.

"At that time there was in that land an old captain who had gone to the ocean many times before. The prince himself went to him and said, 'I need you to come as guide for the voyage to the ocean, so get ready.' The captain said, 'Besides being old and frail, I cannot see with my eyes, so I am not able to go.'

"Then the prince went to his father and said to the king, 'There is an old captain who has gone to the ocean many times before and knows the ocean well. Please command him to be my guide for the voyage to the ocean.' The king himself went to the blind captain and instructed him: 'The prince wants to go to the ocean. Though we gave him such counsel as that he must not go, he would not listen. You go as the prince's guide for the voyage to the ocean.' The captain replied, 'Besides being old and frail, both my eyes are blind; yet if the king commands, I shall not disobey his command.'

"When the king came back to the palace, he asked, 'Who is there who dares to go to the ocean with my son?' And Prince Evil Purpose said, 'I too will go, as my elder brother's servant.' The king thought, 'It is better for his younger brother to go than for others to go,' and he allowed the younger brother to go as well.

"Then the prince brought out three thousand gold coins. He spent a thousand coins on the ship's fittings and two thousand on supplies needed for the voyage, and set out. The king, the queens and all the others saw him off.

"Then they reached the ocean shore, boarded the ship and moored it with seven ropes. Waiting for the wind, he said to the merchants who were going, 'In the ocean there are all kinds of obstacles. Many have set out in the past, but very few have returned. If any of you has doubts about this, turn back. If, without regard for your body and life, and without attachment to your parents, children, wives, kin and friends, you reach the Isle of Jewels and return home safely, then down to the seventh generation of your children and grandchildren you will not be in want of wealth.' Having told them this, he cut one rope. In the same way, for seven days, he told them this once each day and cut one rope each time. Then, when the wind came from the right direction, they hoisted the sail and sped off swift as an arrow.

"When they reached the Isle of Jewels, the prince, being highly skilled in assessing jewels, showed them which jewels were good and which bad, and how many of each to take, and each of them searched for jewels on his own. He and the captain boarded a small boat, leaving most of the merchants there.

"As they went on, the captain asked, 'Now a white mountain should be appearing. Don't you see it?' The prince said, 'Something like that does appear.' He said, 'That is the silver mountain.' Going on again, he asked, 'Now a blue mountain should be appearing. Do you see it?' He said, 'Something like that does appear.' He said, 'That is the vaiḍūrya mountain.' Going on again, he asked, 'Now a yellow mountain should be appearing.' He said, 'Something like a yellow mountain does appear.' He said, 'That is the golden mountain.'

"Then they reached the foot of the golden mountain, and as they sat on the golden sand, the captain said, 'I am old and worn out, and now I cannot go any farther. I am going to die right here. Go on from here, and ahead you should come upon a fortress of the seven precious things. If the fortress gate is shut, there should be a vajra pestle by the gate. Strike the fortress gate with the vajra pestle, and five hundred goddesses will come out from within, each carrying jewels to offer you. Then one goddess will come before you carrying a blue jewel. That is the wish-fulfilling jewel. As soon as you obtain it, hold it tight and do not lose it. Take the jewels of the other maidens too. Restrain your senses well, and do not speak with them. I am exhausted, and when I die here, remember my kindness: bury my bones right here, and go on.' As soon as he had said this, he died.

"Then the young captain buried the bones in the golden sand and went on as he had been told, and before long he reached the fortress of the seven precious things. The gate was firmly shut, so he took up the vajra pestle beside it and struck the gate. The gate opened by itself, and the five hundred goddesses came out one after another, each carrying jewels, and offered them to him. One goddess from within, the first of them, held a blue jewel in her hand. He took it as he had been told, tied it into the hem of his robe, and turned back at once.

"While they were apart from Prince Good Purpose, Prince Evil Purpose told the merchants, 'Take plenty of jewels!' So the merchants took a great many, and because the jewels were so many the ship went under in some places and came up in others. Prince Good Purpose alone did not sink in the water, because he had the precious wish-fulfilling jewel. Prince Evil Purpose was terrified and said to Good Purpose, 'Save me too!' So Good Purpose took Evil Purpose with him and crossed the ocean.

"When they reached dry land, Evil Purpose said to his elder brother, 'We two brothers left our parents and went to the ocean to fetch jewels, but we had no luck. To go home without jewels, empty-handed, is shameful.'

"Prince Good Purpose, who was straightforward by nature, said to his younger brother, 'I have obtained a jewel, so why be ashamed?'

"'Please show me what sort of jewel you found,' said the younger, and the elder untied the jewel and showed it to him. When the younger brother saw the jewel he thought, 'Even before, although we are both alike their sons, my parents loved my elder brother and did not love me. On top of that, we two went together to fetch jewels, and my elder brother has found a jewel while I have found nothing at all, so if I come home now my parents will love me even less. I will kill my elder brother while he is asleep and take the precious jewel away with me. If my parents ask, I will say, "My elder brother drowned in the ocean."'

"With this in mind he said to his elder brother, 'Since we two brothers are carrying jewels, it is not right for both of us to sleep at the same time. Let us sleep in turns and keep watch.' The elder brother said, 'Let us do so,' and the two brothers kept watch in turns.

"The elder brother kept watch first, and even when his watch had come round, the younger would not get up for a long while. Only after the elder brother had kept watch for a long time did the younger get up. The elder brother, having sat up so long, was drowsy, and after he had fallen asleep the younger took two long thorns from the grove. While his elder brother lay asleep he drove a thorn into each of his eyes, then took the jewel and fled.

"Good Purpose was startled and cried out, 'Robbers are here!' but no one answered. Then a tree goddess said, 'Your robber is Evil Purpose.'

"Then Good Purpose, unable to bear the pain in his eyes, crying aloud and crawling, went on and on until he came near the land of Lishiba, and there he met a cowherd tending some five hundred cattle. A bull among those cattle saw the blinded prince and licked the prince's eyes with its tongue. Then all the cattle gathered there, and the herdsman came there too. He saw a man rolling on the ground, and when he looked closely he saw thorns stuck in his eyes. He gently drew out the two thorns and led him to his home. Then he smeared the wounds with milk and oil, nursed him with food, and cared for him.

"Some time later, when the wounds had partly healed, the prince said, 'Now I will go,' but the cowherd would not let him go. Then, some time after that, when the wounds had healed well, he said, 'Now I will go to the city,' and at last the cowherd escorted him to near the city. The prince said, 'Give me a musical instrument called a kima,' and the cowherd gave him a kima.

"Then the prince played music and sang songs and sweet verses. Everyone listened to him, and all of them brought him food and drink. Five hundred beggars, too, were kept alive by relying on the prince.

"Later a keeper of that king's pleasure garden came there. He heard the sweet music and was delighted, and he asked, 'If you played music in my pleasure garden and also guarded the pleasure garden, could you not do that?'

"'I am blind, so I cannot,' he said.

"The keeper said, 'Even though you cannot see, that is all right. Tie bells to a cord at the tops of the trees, and whenever you hear the cries of birds, just pull the cord. Let us go to my pleasure garden.' So the prince stayed in that garden and played music.

"At that time Evil Purpose came back, and the king asked him, 'Where are your elder brother and the other merchants?'

"He said, 'My elder brother Good Purpose and the other merchants found a great many precious jewels and sank in the water along with the ship. I know how to swim, so I alone escaped.'

"The king his father and the queen were both stunned and fell to the ground. The other people living in the land also heard this, were astounded, and were overwhelmed with grief. The king his father and the queen, their hearts tormented by grief, said, 'Evil Purpose, what harm would it have done if you had gone with your elder brother too?'

"When Prince Good Purpose had lived at home, he had raised a goose. The king said to it, 'Prince Good Purpose went to the ocean to fetch jewels, and they say he sank in the ocean and is lost. Go and look for him and search for him.' He wrote a letter and tied it around its neck, and it set off. The goose flew everywhere, searching and searching, until in that pleasure garden it heard the prince's voice. It alighted there, looked, and found the prince staying there. The prince, who knew the goose's call, untied the letter from its neck and had it read to him. Then he wrote a letter in reply and sent word in detail of how Evil Purpose had driven thorns into his eyes and fled.

"At that time the king of Lishiba had a daughter rare in all the world. The princess was strolling in the pleasure garden when she came upon Prince Good Purpose. She saw Prince Good Purpose, his hair matted, his face full of tears, dressed in poor clothes and sitting at the foot of a tree, and she fell in love with him. Neither of them went anywhere, and she stayed beside him and talked with him.

"When the morning had passed, the king sent for his daughter. She said, 'Bring my meal here. I will not go there.' So the king sent her food, and the princess said to the prince, 'Let us two eat together.'

"The prince said, 'I am a blind beggar, so it is not fitting for me to eat with the king's daughter. If the king heard of it, I would be punished, so it is not fitting.'

"The princess said, 'If you will not eat with me, then I will not eat either.' She said this many times, and then they ate together. They talked pleasantly, and the princess grew so attached to him that she would not go anywhere else.

"Then, as the sun was about to set, the king sent word: 'Daughter, come back!' The princess sent back this reply: 'I am happy to be the wife of this keeper of the pleasure garden, and I want no one else at all. Father, my king, I beg you not to turn me from this intention.'

"When the king heard the message she sent, he could not bring himself to refuse his daughter, but he said, 'This is most astonishing. King Jewel Armor has asked for this girl as a bride, and before his prince has come back from the ocean she is happy to be the wife of a beggar like this. She disgraces my lineage, and I cannot even lift my head. This is astonishing indeed!' And he sent once more to call her, but the princess did not go back on her earlier words and would not come.

"Then the king, out of love for his daughter, could not refuse her. He welcomed the two of them, brought them into the palace, and joined them as husband and wife.

"After many days had passed, the princess was always going out during the day and coming back at night, and the prince grew suspicious. He said to his wife, 'You joined with me as husband and wife, saying you would be my wife, yet during the day you are always going off, and you come back only at night. Seeing how inconstant this is, I am sure you have your mind on someone else.'

"The princess swore, 'I think of you alone; I have not even a hair's breadth of any other thought. If the words I have spoken are true, by this speaking of the truth may one of your eyes see!' The moment she said this, one of his eyes could see.

"Then, as they talked of these things, she asked the prince, 'Where are your parents and your country?'

"The prince said to his wife, 'Have you heard of a king called Jewel Armor?'

"His wife said, 'I have heard of him.'

"'Well then, he is my father. Have you heard of one called Prince Good Purpose?'

"His wife said, 'I have heard of him.'

"'Well then, the one called Prince Good Purpose is I.'

"His wife was startled and asked, 'Then why have you come to such misery?' The prince told her in detail how it had all happened, and the princess said to the prince, 'That Prince Evil Purpose put out your eyes is something unheard of anywhere in the world. I wonder what you would do if you met him.'

"The prince said, 'Evil Purpose did wrong in putting out my eyes, but I bear him not the slightest ill will, nor do I hold a grudge.'

"His wife said, 'I find that very hard to believe. To say you bear no ill will toward one who did you such harm is not something that can be said.'

"The prince said, 'I have not even the tip of a hair of ill will toward Prince Evil Purpose. If these words of mine are true, by these words of truth may my other eye see too!' As he said this, the other eye too saw clearly.

"So the prince could see with both eyes, and his features and form far surpassed those of others. The princess was overjoyed and went to where her father the king was, and said, 'Father, my king, do you know Prince Good Purpose, the son of King Jewel Armor?'

"The king said, 'I know him.'

"'Then would the king wish to see him?'

"'Where is that prince now? I will look at him,' he said.

"The princess replied, 'He is none other than the one who is now my husband.'

"The king laughed at his daughter and said, 'Has this girl gone mad? Or has she lost her memory? What is wrong with her? That prince has not come back from the ocean, and she says a beggar is Prince Good Purpose!'

"The princess spoke again: 'If you do not believe it, please examine him.' So the king went to look, and it was indeed the prince. Laying himself face down on the ground, he said, 'I was at fault in not recognizing you, and I regret it.'

"Then that king secretly sent Prince Good Purpose out onto a road and proclaimed that Good Purpose had only just come back from the ocean. With his ministers he welcomed Prince Good Purpose and brought him to the palace, and he proclaimed that only now had he given him his daughter as his wife.

"At that time the goose also went back to its own country with the letter. When the letter was read, they learned that Prince Good Purpose was alive, and Evil Purpose was put in prison. A messenger was sent to the king of Lishiba as well, with this message: 'I hear that my son Good Purpose came to your land and is living there in misery. You did wrong not to send me word before. Now escort him back with a great company of elephants and horses. If you do not escort him, I shall come myself.'

"At that time Prince Good Purpose remembered the kindness of the cowherd. Afterward he petitioned the king of Lishiba and gave the cowherd many rewards, elephants, horses, clothing, a house, fields, pasture, gold, silver, jewels, male servants, female servants, laborers and so on, and so made him happy and comfortable for the rest of his life.

"Then King Jewel Armor's messenger arrived there, and the king of Lishiba adorned five hundred elephants with jewels. He also sent five hundred attendants, five hundred young maidservants, and some five hundred chariots, and the king himself, together with his ministers and a retinue of many hundreds of thousands playing all kinds of music, escorted his daughter.

"The messenger went on ahead, and King Jewel Armor, together with the queen and his retinue, came out to welcome the prince. When they met the prince, he got down from his mount and bowed to his parents. They took him by the hands, asked after his health, and went to the palace with music playing.

"Then Prince Good Purpose said, 'Where is Evil Purpose?'

"'He is now in prison,' they said.

"Then he said, 'Release Prince Evil Purpose!'

"'He has done a great wrong, so it is not right to release him,' the king ordered.

"'If Prince Evil Purpose is not released, then I will not go into the palace either,' he said, and so Evil Purpose was released.

"Then Prince Evil Purpose too came to welcome his elder brother, bowing to him, so it is told. The two brothers embraced each other, and the elder came to love his younger brother even more than before, so that everyone held it a wonder.

"Then, when they had come into the palace, he gently asked, 'Where is the jewel?' and the younger said, 'I hid it in a hole I dug in the ground along the road I came by.'

"Then they sent people to fetch it, but they could not find it, until Evil Purpose went along with Good Purpose, and as soon as they reached the place Good Purpose saw it and found it. Then, having found the jewels, he came back to the palace and gave one to each of the five hundred vassal kings. Keeping only one for himself, he held it in his hand and made this aspiration: 'If this jewel is truly the precious wish-fulfilling jewel, may the throne on which my parents sit become a throne of the seven precious things, and may there also be a parasol of the seven precious things!' As soon as he had said this, it came about just so.

"Then he spoke again: 'From the king's treasury and the ministers' treasuries I formerly gave gifts. May every kind of loss be filled again!' And merely by his pointing the jewel in their direction, all the treasuries became completely full of jewels.

"Then a bell was sounded and it was proclaimed: 'Seven days from now Prince Good Purpose will bring down a rain of the seven precious things. Let everyone gather!' Prince Good Purpose washed his body with scented water and put on new, clean clothes. He fastened the precious wish-fulfilling jewel to the top of a victory banner, took a censer in his hand, bowed to the four directions, and said, 'If this jewel is truly the precious wish-fulfilling jewel, let it rain down for everyone whatever they wish, just as they wish!'

"As soon as he had said this, clouds rose up from the four directions, and the wind of the clouds swept away all that was unclean. First a fine drizzle fell and laid the dust. Then food of a hundred flavors, of every kind, rained down. Then garments rained down. Then the seven precious things rained down and filled the whole land. At that time everyone came to regard jewels and gold as all the same, and then the prince commanded them: 'Now that you have obtained all you need, just as you wished, you will no longer be in want or toil. Each of you, restrain your body, speech and mind, and strive in the ten paths of virtuous action.' All of them did as they were commanded and devoted themselves to the ten virtues. Because they did no evil, when they passed from that life they were born as gods in the higher realms.

"Ānanda, the one who at that time, on that occasion, was King Jewel Armor is now my father, Śuddhodana. The king's queen is now my mother, Lovely Goddess. Prince Good Purpose is now I myself. The king of Lishiba is now Kāśyapa. The one who at that time became the prince's wife is now Prajāpatī. Prince Evil Purpose is now Devadatta. The people of Jambudvīpa whom my kindness reached then are the eighty thousand sons of the gods who came when I first attained buddhahood, and my close disciples who have received prophecies.

"Ānanda, if at that time, on that occasion, I gave rise to a mind of tender love for him even though he did me such great harm, then now that I have become fully awakened, my obscurations of the afflictions exhausted and great compassion pervading everything far and wide, what need is there to speak of my tender love for one who has done me some small harm?"

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, some of the great assembly attained stream-entry, the stage of non-return, and arhatship. Some became pratyekabuddhas, and some gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. When he had spoken thus, all rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the chapter on Prince Good Purpose, the thirty-third.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, together with a great Saṅgha of one thousand two hundred and fifty monks.

At that time there were in that land five hundred beggars who always lived by depending on the Buddha and the Saṅgha. Later those beggars thought, "Now we will go forth in the Buddha's teaching." They went to where the Buddha was and said to the Blessed One, "We have been kept alive by the compassion of the Buddha and the Saṅgha. Let us go forth!"

To this the Blessed One replied, "The Dharma I teach is utterly pure; in it there is no high or low, no rich or poor. It is like water: high and low, rich and poor, good and bad, it washes them all, and there is nothing that is not made clean when washed with water. It is like fire: among mountains and cliffs and between heaven and earth, there is nothing, great thing or small, that it does not burn. Moreover, my Dharma is like space: men, women, boys, girls, the rich, the poor, whoever they may be, there is no one it does not take in. You are permitted to practice it as you wish."

When the five hundred beggars heard this, they were delighted, joyful and glad, and said, "We wish all the more to go forth under the Buddha." The moment the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" their hair and beards fell away of themselves, and they became monks wearing saffron robes and received full ordination. When the Blessed One taught them the Dharma, their bodies and minds were fully liberated, their defilements were exhausted, and they became arhats.

At that time the householders and merchants of that land, men of great and high family, heard that the Buddha had ordained beggars, and they looked down on this and scorned it. They said, "If we invite the Blessed One and the noble Saṅgha and make merit, how could we seat those low-caste beggars he has ordained on our cushions and let them touch our vessels?"

At that time Prince Jeta invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha to a meal and said to the Blessed One, "I invite the Buddha and the Saṅgha, but not the beggars who have newly entered."

The next day, when mealtime had come, the Buddha and the Saṅgha were invited and set out, and the Blessed One said to the beggar monks who had not been invited, "Since the patron has not invited you, go today to Uttarakuru, gather the rice that grows there without seed being sown or ground being plowed, and come to the patron's house to eat it."

As they had been told, those monks went by the miraculous power of arhats to Uttarakuru, filled their alms bowls with rice, and, with graceful bearing, came through the sky above like kings of the geese to the house of Prince Jeta. They sat down in order and ate the rice.

At that time Prince Jeta saw those monks come with graceful bearing through the sky above by miraculous power. He was filled with joy and amazed, and he said to the Blessed One, "From what quarter have these noble ones come, endowed with such splendor and such qualities, arriving with such graceful bearing?"

The Blessed One said to Prince Jeta, "If you wish to know about them, listen well, and I will explain it to you. These monks are the monks whom you, prince, did not invite. Because you did not invite them, each of them fetched rice from Uttarakuru and is eating it."

When Prince Jeta heard him say this, he was deeply ashamed and regretful. "I was blinded by delusion and did not recognize the noble ones," he said, and then he said to the Blessed One, "The qualities of the Blessed One are inconceivable. These beggars were of the very lowest and meanest family in this land, yet here they have gained happiness in this world and have also found their lasting aim. The Blessed One came into the world for the sake of these aims alone. Further, Blessed One, what roots of virtue did these beggars guard in the past, and in what meritorious deeds did they exert themselves, that they have met the Blessed One and become liberated? And what evil did they do, that from birth they were born as beggars and became poor and destitute?"

The Blessed One said to Prince Jeta, "Long ago, beyond immeasurable, countless, inconceivable eons past, here in Jambudvīpa, at the place called Ṛṣipatana in the city called Vārāṇasī, there dwelt many pratyekabuddhas. Even when no buddha had come into the world, pratyekabuddhas lived there, and when there were no pratyekabuddhas on that mountain, sages with the five superknowledges lived there.

"At that time two thousand sages were living there. Later the soothsayers foretold, 'For twelve years no rain will fall.' At that time there was in that land a rich householder of great wealth called Harmonizer, and the sages went to him and said to the householder, 'Are there food and drink for us, these thousand sages who have gone forth, for twelve years?'

"He said, 'We have them, so I beg you noble ones to stay and not go elsewhere.'

"Then the other thousand sages also came and said to the householder Harmonizer, 'We need food and drink for twelve years.' The householder asked his treasurer, 'Is there enough to sustain these noble ones for twelve years?' The treasurer replied, 'There is enough.' So the householder appointed five hundred workers to provide for the two thousand sages and set them to preparing alms food.

"After a time the workers grew weary of the long labor of cooking the alms food, and they said, 'The reason we are always so miserable like this is these beggars.'

"At that time the householder appointed one worker to issue the invitation every day at mealtime. This inviter had a dog, and the dog always went along behind the inviter. One day the inviter forgot, and at mealtime the dog went to the sages and barked loudly. When the sages heard the dog barking, they knew that mealtime had come, and they went for alms.

"Some time later, after they had taken their meal, the sages said to the householder, 'Householder, the rain will come now, so have the farmers do the work of the fields.' The householder had the farmers do the work of the fields, and they sowed a great deal of barley, wheat and other grain. Some time later every kind of grain they had sown grew up as gourds. The householder was amazed and said to the sages, 'Will these not come to nothing?'

"The sages said, 'They will not come to nothing. The rain will fall in due season, and so on; do the work.'

"Not long afterward all the gourds had grown large. When they split them open and looked, whatever had been sown had grown within them, and every gourd was full of it. The householder was overjoyed and delighted, and all his granaries were filled.

"When the five hundred workers saw these things, regret arose in them. 'We reviled the noble ones with harsh words,' they said, and confessed it. And each of them made this aspiration: 'May we too meet noble ones in a future time and become liberated!'

"Because these five hundred beggars spoke harsh words, for five hundred lives they were always beggars. Because they confessed and made the aspiration, they have met me and reached the end of saṃsāra.

"Prince, you should understand it in this way. The one who at that time, on that occasion, was the rich householder Harmonizer was I. The treasurer was Prince Jeta. The man who gave the invitation each day was King Udrāyaṇa. The dog at that time was the householder Sweet Voice; by the power of that deed he has had a sweet voice in all his births. The five hundred men who were workers at that time are these five hundred arhats."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, some of the great assembly attained the first fruit, and some attained up to the fourth fruit. Some gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. They rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the householder Harmonizer, the thirty-fourth.

The tenth section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. He was teaching the true Dharma to a great many people.

At that time all the people of the city were setting out to hear the Dharma. In that town a blind brahmin was sitting by the roadside, and as many people went past he asked, "Where are you going in such a hurry?"

The people said, "Have you not heard? A tathāgata has appeared in the world, and he is now staying in this land and teaching the Dharma. Since it is very hard to meet a tathāgata who has appeared in the world, we are going to hear the Dharma from the Buddha."

That brahmin was highly skilled in the eight kinds of voice; merely from someone's speaking, he could tell their fortune and how long they would live. What are the eight? A voice like a sparrow's, a voice like a crow's, an ambiguous voice, an elephant's voice, a drum's voice, a thunder voice, a bell's voice, and the voice of Brahmā. These are the eight.

One who speaks with a voice like a sparrow's is shameless and ungrateful. One who speaks with a voice like a crow's is angry and fierce, delights in killing, and is without compassion. The ambiguous voice is when a man speaks with a woman's voice or a woman speaks with a man's voice; such a person has no fortune, and is poor and base. One who speaks with a voice like an elephant's is upright by nature and intent on helping friends. One who speaks with a voice like a drum's is eloquent, quick-witted and learned, and becomes a king's support. One who speaks with a voice like thunder has a wise mind and is skilled in the Dharma. One who speaks with a bell's voice is rich and wealthy and owns many hundreds of thousands of gold coins. One who speaks with the voice of Brahmā has great merit and great fortune: if he stays in the household, he becomes a wheel-turning king, and if he goes forth, he will without doubt awaken as a buddha.

At that time the brahmin said to the people, "I am highly skilled in voices. If he is a true buddha, he must have the voice of Brahmā. Take me along with you, and I will go too." So they led the blind brahmin along, and as they came near that place he heard the voice of the Buddha speaking. The blind man was overjoyed and delighted, and faith arose in him. Both his eyes opened of themselves, and he saw the Buddha's golden-colored body, adorned with the thirty-two marks, shining brilliantly like the light of the sun. He bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, and boundless joy arose in him. When the Blessed One taught him the Dharma and gladdened him, two hundred thousand evil, nonvirtuous deeds were exhausted. He attained the fruit of stream-entry, came to possess the eye of wisdom, and asked to go forth in the teaching. Then the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and he became a monk. When he was taught the Dharma suited to him, he became an arhat.

The great assembly was full of wonder, and the venerable Ānanda rose from his seat, joined his palms, and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, you work for the good of many beings, and among all those blinded within your compassion, your compassion has been especially great for this blind brahmin. This one blind brahmin has gained both the eye of flesh and the eye of wisdom."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only at this time that I have given eyes to this blind brahmin. In a past time too I gave him eyes."

Ānanda said, "Blessed One, please explain fully what kind of eyes you gave him in that past time."

The Blessed One said, "Long ago, beyond immeasurable, countless, inconceivable eons past, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land called Pukalada, there was a king called Eye-Opener. Because that king could see for up to forty leagues unobstructed even by rocks and walls, he was named Eye-Opener, and he ruled over all the eighty-four thousand vassal kings of Jambudvīpa. That king had twenty thousand queens and palace women, and some five hundred princes. His regent was called Excellent Discipline.

"Because that king ruled in accordance with the Dharma and regarded all beings as if each were his only child, the winds and rains never failed their seasons, the harvests were good, and everyone was happy and content.

"Then one day, in a solitary place, the king thought, 'By the merit of a former life I have become a king of men. I possess precious jewels and great wealth, and everyone heeds my word, just as soft grass all lies down in one direction when the wind blows on it. If in this life I do not also produce the fruit for the next, I will come to suffering. Just as all farmers who sow much in spring have much to gather in autumn, so if I do not sow seed in the spring of this life, I will have no autumn harvest in the next. So it is not right for me to be lazy now. Let me sow seed abundantly in the field of merit!'

"Having thought this, he said to his ministers, 'Bring out all my treasuries, pile everything up at the city gates and at the crossroads where many people gather, and give it to many beings. And let the eighty-four thousand vassal kings also give from all their treasuries, as people wish.' Having so commanded, he raised a golden victory banner. A great bell was sounded and it was proclaimed: 'Let the śramaṇas and brahmins of Jambudvīpa, the poor and destitute, and all the old and the sick gather! Whatever each of them wants will all be given, just as they wish.'

"At that time all the subjects rejoiced in the king who acted according to the Dharma, and they praised him and spread his fame. But among the border vassal kings there was one called Dabarne who did not heed that king's commands and ruled contrary to the Dharma. That vassal king had a wise and intelligent minister called Lodara, and the minister said to the king, 'Your Majesty is short-tempered and fierce, and acting without forethought or reflection, you will come to regret it later. King Eye-Opener is greatly compassionate, and his kindness extends to all the people of Jambudvīpa. Since all the kings heed his commands, it is not right for Your Majesty alone to defy him. If you heed his commands, your children and grandchildren will be happy for generations.' But the king flew into a rage at the minister and would not heed his counsel. Then the minister, too, grew displeased and thought,

"The king acts against the Dharma and improperly, and when I gave him beneficial counsel he grew angry and would not listen. All his subjects are displeased with this king, so he must be done away with." Having thought this, he laid a plot, but the secret got out, and the king sent troops back to have him seized. The minister, sensing this, mounted a swift horse and fled. The army pursued him from behind, and when they caught up with him, the minister, being a skilled archer, at once shot fifteen men with arrows and killed them. Then, since the pursuers could not catch him again, the minister went on to the land of Eye-Opener. He bowed to King Eye-Opener and spoke with him, and the king, pleased, made him a minister as well.

Then he reported, "That king does not heed your commands." The king asked his ministers, "Is that king not under my authority?" and they answered, "Your Majesty has no authority over him." Then the minister mustered the armed forces and asked leave to bring that king down, and the king granted it. Then the finest of the troops were assembled, and they fixed the day on which they would march to the land of Dabarne.

At that time that king, too, heard that an army was coming to his land, and he was deeply distressed. He took it to heart and did not know what to do; he put on soiled clothes and shut himself in a dark chamber. At that time there was a wicked brahmin in that land, and he asked the king, "Why are you so unhappy that you grieve like this?" The king said, "Have you not heard? My minister Lodara fled and went to the land of King Eye-Opener, and they say he is leading an army back against my land. That is why I am unhappy. If he leads an army here, we too will be destroyed." The brahmin said, "Gather the ministers and let us take counsel."

Then the ministers and the brahmin assembled and deliberated, and the brahmin said, "I have heard that King Eye-Opener is attached to nothing at all apart from his own father and mother, and that he practices generosity. In this land there is a blind brahmin. Let us send him to that king to beg for one of his eyes. If he gives the eye, he will not lead the army here." Having so deliberated, they put it to the king, and the king said, "Let it be done so," and sent for the blind brahmin.

When the blind brahmin had come, the king spoke to him thus: "There is something I ask you to accomplish for me, and you must do it." The brahmin replied, "Since both my eyes are blind, what is there that I could accomplish?" The king commanded, "King Eye-Opener has arrayed his armed forces and is coming here. I am young, so even if I flee I may escape; but you, without eyes, have nowhere at all to flee. It is said that when that king makes a gift, he gives in accord with the other's wish. Go and ask him for his eyes. If he gives them, the army will not come. Go and ask him for his eyes!" The brahmin said, "If I cannot see, how am I to do it?" The king commanded, "Go! I will provide provisions for the road, a guide, and whatever other necessities you need. If you obtain the eyes, I will also give you a great reward." So commanded, he set out.

At that time various kinds of evil omens appeared in the land of King Eye-Opener. That is to say, the sky seemed to break apart and thunderbolts fell; stars fell and the sky grew dark; thunderbolts struck and the whole earth cracked open; birds uttered weeping cries in the sky and fell to the ground; and lions, tigers and wolves uttered piteous cries, came among the people and rolled on the ground. Such various evil omens appeared.

Then the blind brahmin by stages reached that land, came to the palace gate, and called out in a loud voice: "I have come here from another land. Your virtues, O king, are renowned everywhere: I have heard that when a beggar comes you give him anything at all, never acting against the beggar's wish. Beset by hardship, I have come here to beg. Give me what I want!" When the king heard these words, he went out to receive him and spoke with words of good cheer: "Were you not wearied on the road? Were you not troubled? Did you come in comfort?" And he said, "Ask for whatever you need, a kingdom, or jewels, or horses, or vehicles, or clothes, or food, or drink, or medicine for sickness, whatever it may be, and I will give you all of it to your satisfaction." The brahmin said, "Giving external material things does not become supreme merit; but if one makes an inner gift, the fruit is great. Since I lost my eyes long ago I am always in darkness and bewildered. I ask for the king's eyes."

When he had said this, the king rejoiced greatly and told the brahmin, "I will give you my eyes." The brahmin said, "If the king is to grant them to me, when will he grant them?" He commanded, "Seven days from now I will give them," and had a bell rung in proclamation and announced it to the eighty-four thousand vassal kings: "Seven days from now King Eye-Opener will make the gift of his eyes to a brahmin. All who wish to see it, come!"

At that time the people of Jambudvīpa, hearing this proclamation, all came there swiftly. All eighty-four thousand vassal kings, too, with their own ministers, came before the king, threw themselves face down on the ground, shed tears, and said, "We, all the people of Jambudvīpa, live in reliance on the king. Why would you abandon us all for the sake of one man?" The ministers, the queens and the palace servants, too, struck their heads on the ground and lay face down on the earth, pleading, "Do not abandon us all for the sake of one brahmin!" and they wailed aloud. The five hundred princes also wept: "We have no protector! We have no refuge! Out of your compassion, please turn your mind back from making the gift of your eyes!"

The regent Excellent Discipline, too, said, "Royal father, please give my eyes as your gift. My death would be of little account, but if my father were to be without eyes, all the people of Jambudvīpa would be without a protector and without an ally." Then the king spoke thus to the vassal kings, the ministers, the queens and the princes:

"Through a long succession of births I have undergone every kind of birth in bodies like this one. The bones I have piled up are higher than Mount Meru. If the blood were gathered, it would be more than the four oceans. The mother's milk I have drunk is greater than the four great rivers, and the tears I have wept at being parted from what I loved are equal to the ocean. As a being in the hells, too, the times I was burned, boiled and had my eyes torn out are past counting. In the realm of the hungry ghosts I underwent bodies of every kind, and countless times my body was set ablaze and burned up, body and eyes together. Born in the wombs of animals, killing one another and dying, I wasted countless bodies. When born among humans, the times I died before my time were many and the times I reached the full end of my life were few, for, attached to desires, people kill and harm one another. There too I squandered countless bodies and eyes. Even if I was born a god, it did not last long. In short, in the six destinies of the three realms, for the sake of attachment, aversion and delusion, I have wasted as many bodies as there are fine particles of dust, yet not once did I practice generosity, and never did I seek the awakening of a buddha. These eyes, utterly foul and filthy, will rot and perish soon enough. Now it is right to give, and not right to withhold. I shall give these eyes of flesh and gain the eye of a buddha, omniscience. When this aspiration of mine is fulfilled, I shall give you, too, the pure eye of the Dharma. Do not obstruct my mind set on unsurpassed awakening!" When he had commanded this, the whole assembly sat without saying a word.

Then he commanded the ministers, "Let one of you put out my eyes." They replied, "Even if our bodies were ground to dust, we would not dare to pull out a single hair of the king, let alone lay a hand on the king's eyes!" The king commanded again, "Then find a man with a dark complexion who glares out from under his brows." Such a fierce-looking man was found, and the king put a knife in his hand and commanded, "Put out my eyes!" He put out the king's eye and placed it in the king's hand.

With the eye placed in his hand, the king made this aspiration: "By this gift of my eye, may I attain complete and perfect awakening! If I am to attain complete and perfect awakening, may the blind brahmin see the instant he receives my eye!" The moment he had made this aspiration, the king's eye was set into the brahmin's eye socket, and at once he could see. He said to the king, "With the king's eye I have come to see. Seeing with this I am content; I do not need the other one." The king commanded, "I promised to give you both my eyes. If I kept one back, I would go against what I said before." And he put out the other eye as well, took it in his hand, and made the same aspiration as before. It was set into the brahmin's eye socket, and the brahmin came to see with both eyes.

At that moment heaven and earth all shook. All the palaces of the gods trembled and swayed. The gods were amazed, and all of them, seeing that a bodhisattva had made the gift of his eyes, came into the sky above, made offerings of divine flowers and incense, and praised him, saying, "Excellent! Excellent!" Śakra (Indra) too came there and spoke thus to the bodhisattva: "What karmic maturation do you seek from such an extremely difficult deed as this?" The bodhisattva said, "I do not seek the happiness of the three realms, such as that of the king of the māras, or Brahmā, or Śakra, or the Four Great Kings, or a universal monarch. Rather, by this merit I would attain full awakening, in order to establish all sentient beings in the bliss of nirvāṇa." Śakra said, "Since putting out your eyes like this was painful, did no regret or anger arise in your mind?" The king said, "There is no regret, and not the slightest trace of anger." Śakra said, "As I look at you, blood is dripping from both your eyes and your complexion is changing, so it is hard to believe you when you say you have no regret." The king said, "If it is true that I have no regret about this gift of my eyes, and that I shall attain unsurpassed awakening, then by this true speech may my two eyes see as before!" The moment he had spoken, his two eyes became even clearer than before, and then all the gods and humans were overjoyed and delighted.

Then the king said to the brahmin, "Through my gift of my eyes you have come to see. When I become a buddha, I shall give you the eye of the Dharma as well. Take these jewels too and go back home." The brahmin, having gained eyes and jewels, went back to his own land, and that king himself came out to meet him and, as soon as they met, asked, "Did you get the eyes?" The brahmin said, "I got the eyes, and as you see, I can see." The king said, "Then is he dead or not?" He said, "Just then the gods came there, and when he made an aspiration his two eyes became clearer than before." When King Dabarne heard him say this, he was stricken with terror, his heart burst, and he died.

"Ānanda, at that time, on that occasion, the king called Eye-Opener was I, as I am now. Dabarne was Devadatta. The brahmin who begged for the eyes is this present blind brahmin who has attained arhatship. Then too I gave him eyes, and now I have given him the eye of wisdom. For your sake, in all my births, I practiced austerities and attained complete awakening. You too, exert yourselves on the path that is to come."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, among the great assembly some attained the state of stream-enterer, once-returner, non-returner and arhat, and some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter of King Eye-Opener, the thirty-fifth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada.

At that time King Prasenajit had a great minister, learned, intelligent, powerful and wealthy. His wife became pregnant and gave birth to a boy endowed with the marks, handsome in form and with features beyond compare. He summoned a soothsayer and showed him the child, and the soothsayer, his face bright with joy, said, "This boy bears the marks of merit. He will excel all others, and he will have a steady mind and a learned intellect." Hearing this, the boy's father asked, "What name should we give the boy?" The soothsayer asked, "Since this boy was conceived, has anything remarkable happened?" The minister said, "Before, this boy's mother was not gentle and mild by nature, but since this boy was conceived she has become exceptionally gentle and mild. She delights in speaking of others' good qualities and excellences, takes no pleasure in speaking of their faults, and has become loving and tender toward those stricken by misfortune." The soothsayer said, "This is the boy's virtue." And they named the boy Ahiṃsaka.

Then he was reared and grew up, and his strength reached the measure of a mighty wrestler. Alone he could overcome a thousand men; with a leap he could catch a bird flying in the sky; and in speed he was a match for a horse. Because of this they cherished and loved the boy all the more.

At that time there lived in that land a brahmin of learned intelligence, widely read and expert in the treatises, and some five hundred pupils were always studying under that brahmin. At that time the minister, too, took his son and entrusted him to the brahmin to be taught. The boy Ahiṃsaka was so diligent in study that what he learned in a single day surpassed what others learned in a year, and before long he had grasped and understood everything. The teacher, the brahmin, placed special confidence in him and was always in his company and sat together with him, so the other pupils too treated him with respect.

At that time the wife of the teacher, the brahmin, saw that the boy was handsome and fine of form, and more accomplished in his studies than the others, and she became attached to him. But although she held him in her mind, he was never apart from the pupils and kept company with them day and night, so there was no chance to speak with him. Since things did not go as she wished, she was constantly tormented in her mind.

Some time later a patron arranged provisions for three months and invited the teacher and his pupils as his guests. At that time the teacher, the brahmin, consulted with his wife, saying, "The patron has invited me together with my pupils and arranged provisions for three months. Now, would it be right to appoint one of the pupils to the work here and leave him behind?" His wife was pleased and said to the brahmin, "The household affairs and the many tasks must be done by someone knowledgeable and capable, so it would be right to leave the boy Ahiṃsaka behind; he is reliable." So the brahmin commanded, "Ahiṃsaka, my boy, stay behind and look after the household affairs and duties. I am going, since the patron has invited me." And the boy Ahiṃsaka stayed behind, as his teacher had commanded.

At that time, after the teacher, the brahmin, had left with his pupils, the brahmin's wife was overjoyed. She adorned herself elaborately and spoke with the boy in seductive ways, but Ahiṃsaka held firm and his mind did not waver. Then, with desire arising in her, she spoke plainly: "I have been drawn to you, but until now there was no opportunity. Now the teacher has gone, and it was on purpose that you were left behind. Now we are alone and the moment has come: give in to my wishes!" Ahiṃsaka said, "I am a brahmin, and by the dharma of brahmins I may not lie with my teacher's wife. If I did such a thing I would be no brahmin. I would rather die; this I dare not do."

Since things had not gone as she hoped, the teacher's wife was ashamed and embarrassed, bore a grudge, and resolved to stir up trouble. When the teacher was about to return, she tore her clothes, scratched her face with her nails, smeared herself with dust, and lay on the ground saying nothing, as though struck dumb. Then the teacher, the brahmin, came back and entered the house, and seeing his wife lying like that he asked, "Why are you in such a state?" His wife shed tears and said, "It is not worth asking." The teacher, the brahmin, pressed her, saying, "Tell me what wrong has been done," and his wife wept and said, "That Ahiṃsaka you always praise kept trying to get me to lie with him after you had gone. When I refused, he tore my clothes and wounded my face. Is this the kind of pupil you have?"

When the brahmin heard this he was furious, and he said to his wife, "This Ahiṃsaka is strong enough to overcome a thousand men by himself, and he is the son of a great minister. Since his family is noble, even if he has done wrong, it must be dealt with by cunning." So saying, he went to Ahiṃsaka, cheered him with pleasant words of every fitting kind, and asked him, "Were you not burdened by the great amount of work after I left?" Then he said this: "From first to last you have cared for me even to the extent of a cup of water, and I always remember your kindness. Now there is a single secret teaching that I have never taught before, and I will teach it to you. If you do accordingly, you will without doubt be reborn as the god Brahmā." The boy Ahiṃsaka knelt on the ground and asked him to teach it, and he said this to Ahiṃsaka: "For seven days keep the virtuous observances; then cut off the heads of a thousand men, take one finger from each, and make a garland for your body of the thousand fingers. Then you will see the god Brahmā face to face, and the instant you die you will be reborn as the god Brahmā." Thus he declared.

When the boy Ahiṃsaka heard his teacher teach this, doubt arose in him, and he said to the teacher, "It is not reasonable that one should be reborn as the god Brahmā by taking life."

The teacher said, "If you are my pupil, why do you not heed the instruction I have taught you? If you do not believe it, there is no need to look ashamed: do not stay here, go wherever you please!" So saying, he cast a secret mantra on him: he planted a sword in the ground and cast the mantra over it, and anger arose in the boy Ahiṃsaka. When the teacher knew he was enraged, he put the sword in his hand. Ahiṃsaka took up the sword, ran out of the gate, killed everyone he met, cut off their fingers and strung them as a garland for his body, so that everyone called him Aṅgulimāla, "Garland of Fingers."

He went on killing everywhere, and on the day the seven days were complete he had gathered nine hundred and ninety-nine fingers; with one more finger the number of those killed would reach a full thousand. But everyone had fled and hidden, so that not a single person was abroad, and though he searched everywhere he found no one. Since he had eaten no food for seven days, his mother, out of love for her son, sent people to take him food, but all were afraid and not one dared. Then the boy's mother herself took the food and went toward her son. When he saw his mother coming from afar, he ran at her, ready to kill her. His mother said to him, "Without any reverence, what perverse thought do you have, that you would commit an act of immediate retribution and kill your mother?" He replied to his mother, "As my teacher taught, I am to make up a full thousand fingers in seven days, and when the thousand fingers are complete I shall be reborn as the god Brahmā. The number of fingers is not complete, and I cannot fail to do it, so now I must kill you." The boy's mother said to him, "If that is so, could you not cut off my finger without killing me?"

At that moment the Blessed One, seeing and knowing from afar that the time to tame him had come, emanated himself as a monk and went toward him. When Aṅgulimāla saw the monk, he let his mother be, leapt at the monk, and made ready to kill him. Seeing him come like this, the Buddha walked on at a leisurely pace, yet though Aṅgulimāla ran with all his might and strength, he could not catch up, and he called out from afar, "Monk, stop a moment!" From afar the Blessed One replied, "I am always still; it is you who are not still." Aṅgulimāla said, "How is it that you are still and I am not still?" The Blessed One declared, "My faculties are always at peace, and I have gained mastery of samādhi, so I am still. Your teacher taught you wrongly, and you believed in what is evil; your mind has been deceived and is not still, so day and night you do the work of killing and commit endless evil."

The moment he heard this, Aṅgulimāla understood and grasped it in his mind. He threw his sword on the ground and from afar bowed down, saying, "I go for refuge." Then the Blessed One went to him, transformed himself into the form of a buddha, and showed himself radiant with brilliant light, clearly adorned with the thirty-two marks. When Aṅgulimāla saw the Buddha's body with its light and its marks, he threw himself to the ground, confessed his wrongdoing and repented, and when the Blessed One taught him the Dharma, the pure Dharma eye arose in him with regard to phenomena. In faith he asked to go forth, and when the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" his hair and beard fell away of themselves and he became a monk. When the Blessed One taught him the Dharma suited to him, all the stains of his mind were exhausted and he became an arhat, and the Blessed One led him to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta.

At that time there was no one among the people and other living creatures dwelling in that land who was not terrified at the mere mention of Aṅgulimāla's name, and pregnant women and pregnant animals were so frightened that they could not give birth. At that time a female elephant could not deliver her young, and the Blessed One said to Aṅgulimāla, "Go to the female elephant and speak these words of truth: 'From the time I was first born until now I have not killed a single person.'" Aṅgulimāla said to the Blessed One, "I have killed many people, so how could I say that I have not killed?" The Blessed One declared, "You are called one newly born from the noble Dharma." Then Aṅgulimāla adjusted his robes, went to the female elephant as the Blessed One had told him, and spoke those words, and she gave birth safely. Aṅgulimāla then returned to his dwelling.

King Prasenajit set out with his armed forces to seize Aṅgulimāla, and the road the troops took passed by the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta. In the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta there was a monk, short of body and ugly, whose voice was exceedingly sweet. When the troops heard him chanting in the temple, they wanted to listen to him without ever tiring, and even the elephants and horses pricked up their ears and listened, unwilling to go on. The king was amazed and asked, "Why are the elephants, horses and the rest unwilling to go on?" They replied, "They will not go on because they want to listen to the sweet voice." The king said, "If even those born in the wombs of animals delight so much in listening to the Dharma, how much more should I, born among humans! I shall listen for a moment." He went to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, got down from his elephant chariot, unbuckled his sword, set aside his parasol, and went into the presence of the Blessed One where he was staying. Having arrived, he bowed his head to the ground and said to the Blessed One, "Who is that monk with the sweet voice? I wish to see him, and I will also offer him forty thousand kārṣāpaṇas." The Blessed One said to the king, "First make the gift, and meet him afterward. If you meet him first, you will not want to give even a single kārṣāpaṇa." Then the king met the monk, and since he was so very ugly and exceedingly short, not even the thought of giving him a single kārṣāpaṇa arose. The king knelt on the ground and said this to the Blessed One:

"Blessed One, what deeds did this monk do in the past, that his body is so ugly and short and yet his voice so exceedingly sweet? How did he come to receive such a karmic maturation?" The Blessed One declared, "Listen well and bear it in mind, and I shall explain.

"Long ago in the past, a buddha called Kāśyapa came into the world. After he had worked for the benefit of beings and passed into nirvāṇa, a king called Kṛkī gathered his relics and set about building a stūpa. Then four nāga kings, taking the form of men, came before the king and asked, 'Will this stūpa you are building for the relics be made of precious substances, or of earth?' The king said, 'A stūpa ought to be built of precious substances, but since there are not many precious substances, it will have to be made of earth. It must be built square, each side five yojanas, and twenty-five yojanas in height, visible from everywhere.' The four nāga kings said to the king, 'We four are nāga kings. Since the king is making a stūpa, we thought that we too would contribute precious substances.' When the king heard them say this, he was overjoyed and delighted and said, 'It is excellent that you offer such help.' And the nāgas said this: 'Outside the four sides of the city there are four great springs. If you draw water from the spring on the eastern side and make bricks with it, they will all turn into blue beryl. If you draw water from the southern spring and make bricks, they will all turn into gold. If you draw water from the western spring and make bricks, they will all turn into silver. If you draw water from the northern spring and make bricks, they will all turn into white crystal.'

"When the king heard them say this, he was all the more overjoyed and delighted. He appointed a master builder for each of the four sides and had each build one side. When the master builders of three sides had finished their parts of the stūpa, the master builder of one side had been lazy, and his part was nowhere near finished. The king himself came to inspect the work on the stūpa and commanded the lazy one, 'Because you have not worked well, it is not finished; you shall be punished.' The man grew angry with the king and replied, 'A stūpa like this is far too big; it will never be finished!' Then, as the king urged them on, everyone worked diligently day and night, and all of it was finished at the same time.

"The stūpa was a wonder to behold, its precious substances glittering and its shape exceedingly fine, so that the master builder of that side who had been angry was also overjoyed. He regretted and confessed his earlier fault, hung a golden bell at the pinnacle of the stūpa, and made this aspiration: 'Wherever I am born, may my voice be exceedingly sweet, and may all beings who hear it delight in it! In a future time, may I meet the Buddha Śākyamuni and be fully liberated from saṃsāra!' Such was the aspiration he made.

"Great king, the master builder of that one side who at that time, on that occasion, was angry and said, 'The stūpa is far too big,' is this monk. Merely because at that time he said in anger, 'The stūpa is far too big,' he has always been short of body and ugly. Because afterward, with a joyful mind, he offered a golden bell, hung it on the stūpa, and made the aspiration, 'May I have a sweet voice and may I meet you,' for five hundred lifetimes his voice has been exceedingly sweet, and now, having met me here, he has attained liberation."

When the king heard him say this, he made ready to go back, and the Blessed One said, "Great king, where are you going?" The king said to the Blessed One, "In this land there is a savage enemy called Aṅgulimāla who has been killing people everywhere and doing harm beyond all measure, so we are going to march against him with an army." The Blessed One declared, "This Aṅgulimāla now does not kill even an ant, so what need is there to speak of killing others?"
