Balamitra is reconstructed from the Tibetan stobs grogs po ("strength-friend"). The mother's name, lha mtshams ma, could not be identified and is translated literally as "Divine Boundary". "Perished" renders the unusual kha dag.  The daughter-in-law is here called by the same name as the woman earlier said to be her mother (lha mtshams ma, rendered Divine Boundary); the text is followed as it stands. "The fruit of once-returning" renders lan cig phyir mi ldog pa'i 'bras bu, an unusual blend of the terms for once-returner and non-returner; non-returning may be intended. "The king did not believe it" renders the awkward double negative thugs ma ches pa med pa, taken in the sense required by the minister's renewed insistence.  The relatives' outcry (རྒྱལ་པོ་ཆེན་པོ་ནི་འདི་ལྟར་མཉེས་པ་ཐམས་ཅད་ཀུན་བསད་ཀྱིས་དམག་པ་སོགས་ལ་དགྲ་བྱ་བར་འདོང་ངོ) is awkwardly worded; it is taken as "though they pleased him, the king killed them all; we will gather an army and make war," reading དམག་པ་སོགས as དམག་དཔུང་བསོགས. Likewise the soldiers' closing words རྒྱལ་པོ་ཆེན་པོས་ཀྱང་མཉེས་ཀྱིས are taken as "let the great king be forgiven," continuing into the next piece. Here རྒྱུད་ལྔ means the five realms of rebirth, not tantra. The pronoun in "she has attained the fruit" follows the context (Divine Boundary's attainment of once-returning); the Tibetan does not state the subject.  The country གསེར་གྱི་ས་ལ (also གསེར་ས) is rendered "Golden Land"; it may correspond to Suvarṇabhūmi, but as it is placed south of Śrāvastī the identification is left open. ཀ་པྱི་ན is taken as Kapphiṇa (Mahākapphiṇa for his son, the later disciple). The vassal kings are first numbered ཁྲི་ཕྲག་སུམ་ཅུ་རྩ་དྲུག (360,000), but those who come as "half" are ཁྲི་བརྒྱད་སྟོང (18,000); both numbers are given as the text has them. Earlier the army is སྟོང་ཕྲག་སུམ་ཅུ་རྩ་དྲུག (36,000) battalions.  "Some attained stream-entry, once-returning or non-returning" renders རྒྱུན་དུ་ཞུགས་པ་ནས་ལན་ཅིག་ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ, an irregular phrase that seems to run together once-returning (ལན་ཅིག་ཕྱིར་འོང) and non-returning (ཕྱིར་མི་འོང). Here རྒྱུད (ལམ་རྒྱུད་ལྔ, མིའི་རྒྱུད, ཡི་དགས་ཀྱི་རྒྱུད) means a realm or class of rebirth, not tantra. The new king བཻ་དཱུརྱ is kept as Vaiḍūrya, as the Tibetan transcribes him; he corresponds to Prasenajit's son known elsewhere as Virūḍhaka.  དྲུག་སྡེ་མ་བསྙེན་དགའ་མོ is taken as "Upanandā of the group of six" (the notorious group of six nuns); the identification of བསྙེན་དགའ་མོ with Upanandā is probable but not certain. ཨུད་པ་ལའི་མདོག is the nun Utpalavarṇā. In "the boy, seeing his mother coming" the phrase མ་འོངས་པ is read as "the mother coming," not "not come." Here ཨུད་པ་ལ is a short form of Utpalavarṇā's name. In her story Utpalavarṇā speaks of herself with the humble ཁོ་མོ, rendered "I."  "Listen attentively" renders ཁྱོད་རྟག་པར་ཉོན་ཅིག, literally "listen constantly," evidently for the usual "listen well." The place རི་དྲང་སྲོང་ལྷགས is Ṛṣipatana (the Deer Park near Vārāṇasī). The short form ཨུད་པ་ལ here refers to the nun Utpalavarṇā (ཨུད་པ་ལའི་མདོག) and is kept as Utpalavarṇā, as in the preceding piece.  The king's name ཤུད་དོ་ལག་གར་ནི is a Tibetan sound-spelling of an Indic name that cannot be confidently reconstructed; it is given here in simple phonetics as Shudolagarni. The rendering of the buddha's name པུར་ཤ as Puṣya is a probable identification.  In the snake story, ཞི་གནས is used in its everyday sense of a place being settled or at peace, not the meditative term calm abiding. གཙང་སྦྱོར, the monk who received the gold, is taken as the monk responsible for arranging the community's meals and supplies.  The wife's name བདེ་བ is translated by its meaning, Bliss, as its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. ཨ་ལམ་པ, the second rākṣasī, is a sound-spelling given in simple phonetics as Alampa. In the closing of the golden vase story, the line "the snake reviled me, and yet I humbled myself toward it" is a plausible reading of a terse sentence that could also mean that the snake humbled itself after reviling.  The city name བ་རུ་ཏེ is a sound-spelling that cannot be identified with confidence and is given in phonetics as Barute. The youth's name སྦྱིན་པ་ཆེན་པོ is translated by its meaning, Great Generosity, in keeping with the rendering Generosity used for this name earlier in the text; its Sanskrit form would be Mahādāna.  "Those the color of mountain water" renders རི་ཆུའི་མདོག་ཅན literally; the class of sea creature meant is not identified. The phrase rendered "it will be over with him at once" (འཕྲལ་དུ་བྱེ་བྲག་ཕྱེ) is idiomatic; the sense appears to be that he would die of fasting. The city name ང་ཤུ་པ is a sound-spelling that could not be identified with an Indian form and is kept in phonetics. Coral renders མུ་ས་ར་གལ་བ (Skt. musāragalva).  "Mountains that look like the color of water" (ཆུའི་མདོག་ལྟ་བུའི་རི) evidently refers to the same hazard rendered in the previous passage as "those the color of mountain water," apparently submerged or water-colored rocks. The bowl he takes in hand (པོག་ཕོར) is presumably an incense or offering bowl.  The "three hundred and thirty thousand leagues" renders སྟོང་ཕྲག་སུམ་བརྒྱ་སུམ་ཅུ literally (thirty-three hundred-thousand). In the last two sentences the Tibetan reads "forty leagues" after the first dipping and "eighty leagues" after "dipping twice"; whether the eighty is cumulative or an additional amount is not stated.  "A hundred thousand leagues" renders དཔག་ཚད་བརྒྱ་སྟོང literally; it may simply mean an elephant of enormous range. "A level plain" renders ག་ལའི་ཐང, whose first element is unclear; it might also be read as a place name.  "Lovely Goddess" (ལྷ་མཛེས) is rendered literally; the name of the Buddha's mother in this identification could not be confidently identified with a Sanskrit form (the usual name of his mother, Māyā, is rendered differently in Tibetan), and ལྷ may here be the royal honorific, giving "the lovely queen". "Mirror Face" (མེ་ལོང་གདོང) is likewise given literally; it may correspond to Sanskrit Ādarśamukha, but this is not certain. The "three wishes" refer to the three conditions Ānanda requested and was granted when he became the Buddha's attendant; the Tibetan does not state the agent of granting. "Ground of non-returning" renders ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པའི་ས, the stage of irreversibility. "League" renders both དཔག་ཚད (yojana) for the wheel and རྒྱང་གྲགས (krośa) for the jewel's range.  The text says twenty thousand pratyekabuddhas came from each of the four directions, but then gives the number honored as forty thousand (བཞི་ཁྲི); both numbers are translated as written. The brahmin's name ཧ་ཡི is a transliteration whose Sanskrit original is uncertain; it is given as "Hayi". "Good Seeker" (ལེགས་ཚོལ) and "Evil Seeker" (ཉེས་ཚོལ) are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms could not be confidently identified. "Called upon the gods" renders the uncertain ལྟ་བརྗོད. The "nun whose defilements were exhausted" is the arhat nun Utpalavarṇā in the tradition, though she is not named here.  "Jewel Armor" (རིན་པོ་ཆེ་གོ་ཆ), "Good Purpose" (དགེ་དོན) and "Evil Purpose" (སྡིག་དོན) are given literally, as their Sanskrit forms could not be confidently identified. In the passage on Jewel Armor's thoughts, the rare phrase དེའུ་རེ་ཚེ་ཟད་པས is taken as "when my life comes to an end". In the opening, ནང་ལ is read as ང་ལ, "me", as the context requires. The beggars' phrase བག་མ་བྱས་པས is taken in its literal sense "having held a wedding"; it may instead be a slip for བག་མེད་པས, "through carelessness".  "Hidden rocks the color of the water" renders the obscure རི་ཆུའི་མདོག་ཅན, literally "those having the color of mountain and water"; it may instead name a kind of sea creature. "Coral" renders མུ་ས་ར་གལ་བ (Skt. musāragalva), whose identity is debated (coral, cat's-eye or a kind of shell).  "The young captain" renders དེད་དཔོན་གྱི་བུ, referring to Good Purpose, who had declared himself captain of the expedition; it could also be read as "the captain's son", but no such son appears in the story. In the old captain's reply, ང་རྒྱལ་པོས is read as "if the king".  ལི་ཤི་བ is given as Lishiba, a sound-rendering of the country name (Chinese Lishiba in the parallel version of this tale); its Indian form is not securely identifiable. ཀི་མ is a stringed instrument (the Chinese version has a zheng, a zither); it is kept as "kima". The phrase about the ship (བར་འགའ་ནི་ནུབ། བར་འགའ་ནི་བྱུང) is rendered literally as going under in some places and coming up in others; it may instead mean that some of the vessels sank. ཕུ་བོ་སྔར་མེལ་ཙེ་བྱེད་པ་ལས་ ... ལངས་སུ་མ་བཏུབ is read with the younger brother as the one who would not rise, as the following sentence requires.  ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ in the list of attainments, between stream-entry and arhatship, is taken here as the stage of non-return; it can also mean irreversibility. The sentence about the people of Jambudvīpa (འཛམ་བུའི་གླིང་གི་མི་ལ་ངའི་དྲིན་གྱིས་ཁྱབ་པ ... ཉེ་གནས་ལུང་བསྟན་པ་རྣམས་ཡིན) is somewhat compressed; it is read as identifying the people who benefited from the jewel rain with the eighty thousand devaputras and the prophesied disciples. The final sentence breaks off the instruction that continues in the next piece.  The householder's name སྡུམས་བྱེད has been translated by meaning as Harmonizer; its Indian form is not certain (it may correspond to a name such as Sandhāna). The householder སྐད་སྙན has likewise been translated by meaning as Sweet Voice. ཀུ་བ is taken as gourds, in which the sown grain was found growing.  The first of the eight voices (བྱ་བང) is rendered as a sparrow's; the exact bird is uncertain. The land པུ་ཀ་ལ་ད is kept as spelled, Pukalada; it may stand for Puṣkalāvatī, but this is not certain. The king's name མིག་འབྱེད is translated by meaning as Eye-Opener, since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence; the vassal king ད་བར་ནེ and the minister ལོ་ད་ར are kept as sound-renderings. In "it is not right for Your Majesty alone to defy him," the verb བསྔོ་བ does not here have its usual sense of dedication; the meaning "oppose, set oneself against" is inferred from the context.  The destination is written pa la da bar ne'i yul; I have rendered it "the land of Dabarne" to match the name used earlier, taking the opening syllables as part of the same place name. The first omen, gnam grum pa, is uncertain and is rendered loosely as the sky seeming to break apart.  The rival king appears here as pa la dbar ne and pa la da bar; I have kept the short form Dabarne used earlier in the text. ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ in the list of fruits is read as non-returner (normally ཕྱིར་མི་འོང་བ).  མི་གདུང་བ ("one who does not harm") is rendered Ahiṃsaka, the name of this youth (later Aṅgulimāla) in the Sanskrit tradition.  "Act of immediate retribution" renders མཚམས་མེད་པའི་ལས (ānantarya), here matricide. བལ་གླང་མོ is taken as a female elephant.  The king's name ཀྲྀ་ཀཱི་བྱི is rendered Kṛkī, the king associated with the Buddha Kāśyapa; the final syllable is not reflected. Since a stūpa of earth could not have the dimensions stated, I keep the text's measurements as given.  The king's name བ་ལ་མ་དར is a phonetic spelling (this sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese); I could not identify the Indian form with confidence (it may reflect Brahmadatta), so it is given as Balamadar. རྐང་བཀྲ, "Speckled Feet," is the standard rendering of Kalmāṣapāda, the hero of this well-known tale; the text's own etymology is kept in the translation.  In the verse, སོ་སོའི་སྐྱེ་བོ་མཐའ་ཐོག་དག is taken as "ordinary beings, from first to last" (མཐའ་ཐོག "end and beginning"); the phrase is terse. The four venomous snakes are the traditional image for the four elements of the body. The king's name here is spelled བ་ལ་དར, slightly differently from བ་ལ་མ་དར (Balamadar) in the previous story; it is a different king in a different tale and is given phonetically as Baladar, since the Indian form could not be identified with confidence.  "A monk who did menial labor" renders གང་ཟག་གི་ལས་བྱས་པ, literally "who did the work of a person," apparently labor as a worker; the text later calls it "the work of the Saṅgha." In the Buddha's words to Maudgalyāyana, རྒྱ་མཚོ་ཆེན་པོ་བཞིའི་ཆུ་འབབ་པའི་རྫིང་རླུང་ཡུག་པས་བུས་ཀྱང is terse; it is taken as two hypotheticals (pouring the four oceans' waters on it, blowing on it with a hurricane), with རྫིང "pond" read as a slip. The future buddha's name མར་མེ་འོད is translated as "Light of the Lamp," since its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. ལན་ཅིག་ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ is unusual wording; it is taken as the state of non-return.  The king's name པ་སི་ལི is given as transliterated (Pasili); I could not securely identify its Sanskrit form. རིན་ཆེན་གཙུག་ཕུད, "Jewel Crest," is rendered Ratnaśikhin, the buddha whom the parallel Chinese version of this story names "Jewel Topknot"; Ratnacūḍa is also possible. Āryamitra and Muni are reconstructions from the Tibetan meaning ("Noble Friend," "Sage"). Dīpaṃkara is prophesied as having "thirty" epithets (མཚན་སུམ་ཅུ), as the text reads; this is probably a slip for "ten" (མཚན་བཅུ), which the parallel prophecy to Muni has a few lines later. ཉོན་མོངས here means physical toil and weariness, not the technical "afflictions." "The fruits from the first to the fourth" are the four fruits of stream-entry, once-return, non-return, and arhatship.  The names ཤི་རི་ཙི (the father) and བ་ཤི་ཙིར (the son) are given as transliterations, Śirici and Vaśicira; I could not securely identify their Sanskrit forms. In the bride's parents' condition, དམག་པ ("army") is read as མག་པ ("son-in-law"), which the context plainly requires. The phrase ནམ་སྐྱོན་ཡང་ཕྱིར་ཁྱིམ་དུ་འོངས is obscure; "at nightfall he would come back home" is a contextual rendering.  The brahmin's name ཕྱིན་ཏེ་ལོ་ཤུ་ཤ appears to be a transliteration; the parallel Chinese version of this story names him Piṇḍola, and I have used that form, though the Tibetan spelling is irregular. རྒྱལ་པོ་མཛེས་པ ("Lovely") is rendered Sundara, a plausible but unconfirmed Sanskrit form. དབྱུག་པ་ཅན ("one with a staff") is Daṇḍin, matching the Chinese parallel. Throughout, དམག་པ ("army") is read as མག་པ ("son-in-law"), as the context requires.  The tree ཤ་ཀོ་ཏ་ཀ is given as transliterated (śakoṭaka); it is presumably the śākhoṭaka tree (Streblus asper). ཡུལ་བྱིན, rendered "was granted land," could also be read more loosely as "was given leave."  The brahmin's name ཤིན་ཙིར is given as transliterated (Śincira); I could not securely identify its Sanskrit form. "The six teachers" are the six non-Buddhist teachers of the Buddha's time.  The monk's name སྦྲང་རྩི་མཆོག is rendered by its meaning, "Supreme Honey"; the honey-offering monkey's monk is known in Sanskrit sources as Madhuvāsiṣṭha, but the Tibetan does not clearly reflect that form. The householder's name དམ་ཤིན་ཙིར and the son's name དན་བྱི་ལ are given as transcriptions because their Indian forms could not be identified with confidence. The phrase རྒྱལ་པོ་གསལ་རྒྱལ་གྱི་ཕ་ཤི་ནས literally reads "the father of King Prasenajit died"; in context it is taken as the boy's father dying and King Prasenajit installing the boy in his place. ལུས་སྨད་ལྔ is taken as the five points of the body (forehead, hands, knees) in prostration.  The prince's name Vaiḍūrya is the same word as the gem vaiḍūrya (here with མཐོན་མཐིང, rendered "lapis lazuli"); the gem is translated, the prince's name kept. སྲང is rendered "ounce," a standard weight for gold and precious substances.  གླང་པོ་སྐྱོང ("elephant keeper") is rendered Hastipāla, the evident Sanskrit form. The phrase "the two brothers Hastipāla" follows the Tibetan, which introduces a brother without naming him. Ajātaśatru's having killed his father is stated in the future-tinged form གསོད་ན in the Tibetan, but the sense is that he kills even his own father.  ལྡོང་རོས is a red mineral pigment (realgar), rendered "red pigment." Here རྣལ་འབྱོར་སྤྱོད་པ refers to the monks' meditative practice, not the Yogācāra school.  The future buddha's name མངོན་པར་ཤེས་པ་བཟང་པོ could not be identified with a Sanskrit form and is translated by meaning. The king's name བན་དུ is taken as Bandhumat, the king associated with the buddha Vipaśyin in tradition. ཆོས་གོས་སྨད་གསུམ is read as the set of three robes.  The father's name, གུས་པ་དེ, could not be identified with a Sanskrit name with confidence and is translated by its meaning, "Respectful". In the Indian versions of this story of Māndhātṛ, the father is Upoṣadha, but the Tibetan here does not render that name. Mūrdhaja ("Born from the Crown") is the Sanskrit name corresponding to སྤྱི་བོ་སྐྱེས, the future wheel-turning king known elsewhere as Māndhātṛ.  Pūrvavideha (ཤར་གྱི་ལུས་འཕགས་གླིང) and Aparagodānīya (ནུབ་ཀྱི་བ་ལང་སྤྱོད) are the standard eastern and western continents; the shortened forms "Videha" and "Godānīya" in the text are kept. The gods' palace is named ཡིད་དུ་འོང་བ, "Delightful"; I have translated the name rather than guess at a Sanskrit form. The number of gates, བརྒྱ་ཕྲག་ཕྱེད་དང་བཅུ་གཉིས, is read as "eleven and a half hundreds", that is 1,150. The phrase ལྷའི་མགྱོགས་བུལ་གྱིས་རྟོགས་སོ ("distinguished by the quickness or slowness of the gods") is terse; the parallel tradition says the human king could be told apart because his eyes blinked while the gods' did not, but the Tibetan is translated as it stands. The buddha ཕུར་ཤ is a sound-spelling, taken here as Puṣya. The text gives four days for the rain of jewels in the king's summary, although the earlier narrative said seven; both are kept as written.  The first fruit, second and third are stream-entry, once-returning and non-returning. The daughter's name སུ་མ་ན is a sound-spelling of Sumanā. The land ཏིག་ཅ་ཤི་རི is taken as Takṣaśilā, as its sound and the Chinese parallel indicate. In the mother's reply, the Tibetan literally reads "your mother does not let you kill wild animals because of not loving you ... but", which contradicts her preceding words; it is understood with the negation carried over ("Nor is it for lack of love"). The phrase རྒྱལ་པོ་ལ་དུས་ཀྱིས་གསོལ is terse and is rendered as the prince taking leave of the king.  The attendant ཡ་ཤེའི་ཀི is a sound-spelling; it is taken as Yaśas, the name of Ānanda's disciple in the parallel version of this story, though the Tibetan form is unusual. The second son's name ནན་ཏན་ཀུ་བ་ཏ could not be identified; it mixes a Tibetan word (ནན་ཏན, "earnest") with what looks like a garbled sound-spelling of a "-gupta" name, and it is given as it sounds. The text calls Upagupta a wandering ascetic and then the Buddha calls him "this brahmin"; both are kept. The text gives the first-born son the name Upagupta, although the boy who is finally given is the third; this is translated as it stands. The buddha's name ཐམས་ཅད་སྐྱོབ is translated by its meaning, "Protector of All", as its Sanskrit form is uncertain.  "The first fruit" is stream-entry. The ten precepts are those of a novice. The "formal act consisting of a motion and three proclamations" renders གསོལ་བ་དང་བཞིའི་ལས (jñapticaturtha karman), the procedure of full ordination. The sixth heaven of the gods is Paranirmitavaśavartin, the realm of Māra. In this section of the text it is Yaśas, not Upagupta, who is said to transform Māra's conjured woman into a skeleton, and the "venerable one" crowned by Māra is left unnamed; both are translated as they stand.  "Sixty cubits high and sixty cubits wide": the Tibetan reads ཁྱད་ཆག, which is not an evident unit; it is taken here, by parallel with the following ཆག་དྲུག་ཅུ་པ and the sixteen cubits (ཁྲུ) of the Buddha's body above, as a slip for a measure of cubits. ཁྲི་ཕྲག་བརྒྱད་ཅུ is read as eighty times ten thousand, i.e. eight hundred thousand.  The king's name དེ་བྱི is a sound-spelling whose Sanskrit original I cannot identify with confidence; it is given in simple phonetics as "Debyi". The lion's name སེང་གེ་ཡི་དམ་བརྟན་པ is translated by meaning as "Firm Vow", since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. ཁར་རྩང་ཚལ is rendered simply as "a certain grove"; the first element is unclear. མདང་སུམ is taken as "last night" (མདང་).  The lion's eight syllables are a sound-spelling of an obscure Sanskrit-like formula; the text itself glosses each part, so they are left in romanization. The sage's name ཤ་མ is given as a plain romanization, "Śama"; its Sanskrit original cannot be identified with confidence. བསྐལ་པ་ཁྲི་ཕྲག་བཅུ is read as ten times ten thousand, a hundred thousand eons.  ཁྱེདསྐྱག་་པ་ལ་ཟ་ཟའམ is evidently a garbled writing of ཁྱེད་སྐྱག་པ་ལ་ཟའམ, "will you eat excrement?", and is translated so. The novice's name ཀྱུན་ཏེ is a sound-spelling; it corresponds to the novice known in the Chinese version as Junti, often identified with Cunda, but this is not certain, so it is given in simple phonetics as Kyunte. The past buddhas are given by their standard identifications: གཙུག་ཏོར་ཅན Śikhin, ལོག་པར་དད་སེལ Krakucchanda, གསེར་ཐུབ Kanakamuni; ཐམས་ཅད་སྐྱོབ (Viśvabhū) is kept as "Protector of All" for consistency with earlier pieces.  ཐོས་པར་དཀའ ("difficult to hear") makes little sense of a beautiful voice; it is read as ཐོས་པར་དགའ, "delighted to hear". In ཚངས་པ་བྱིན, ཚངས་པ is the confession or purification just made (མཐོལ་ཚངས), not the god Brahmā.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
